The Formal Theory of Monads, Univalently
Niels van der Weide ORCID:0000-0003-1146-4161

Abstract
. We develop the formal theory of monads, as established by Street, in univalent foundations. This allows us to formally reason about various kinds of monads on the right level of abstraction. In particular, we define the bicategory of monads internal to a bicategory, and prove that it is univalent. We also define Eilenberg-Moore objects, and we show that both Eilenberg-Moore categories and Kleisli categories give rise to Eilenberg-Moore objects. Finally, we relate monads and adjunctions in arbitrary bicategories. Our work is formalized in Coq using the UniMath library.



Feb. 06, 2024Feb. 19, 2025

†
†
lmcs-doi:
Volume 21, Issue 1, Paper 16

1.
Introduction

Monads are ubiquitous in both mathematics and computer science, and many different kinds of monads have been considered in various settings.
In functional programming, monads are used to capture computational effects
[
JonesW93
]
.
Strong monads have been used to provide semantics of programming languages such as Moggi’s computational
\lambda
-calculus
[
Moggi89
,
moggi1991notions
]
and models of call-by-push-value
[
levy2012call
]
.
Monads are also used in algebra to represent algebraic theories, and in fact, the class of algebraic theories is equivalent to a class of monads
[
hyland2007category
]
.
This result has been adapted to the enriched case as well in order to relate various notions of computation with enriched monads
[
EggerMS14
,
plotkin2002notions
,
plotkin2003algebraic
,
power1999enriched
]
.
Comonads, the dual notion of monads, found applications in the semantics of linear logic
[
Benton94
,
mellies2009categorical
]
.

A general setting in which all these different variations of monads can be studied, has been developed by Street
[
street1972formal
]
.
This setting, known as
the formal theory of monads
, uses the fact that the notion of monad can be defined internal to an arbitrary bicategory
[
10.1007/BFb0074299
]
, including 2-categories (which were used by Street).
Each of the aforementioned kinds of monads is actually an instance of this more general notion.
For example, monads in the bicategory of symmetric monoidal categories are symmetric monoidal monads, and strong monads are monads in the bicategory of so-called left actegories
[
capucci2022actegories
]
.
Comonads in a bicategory
\mathsf{B}
are the same as monads in
\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{co}}
, which is
\mathsf{B}
with the 2-cells reversed.
Even several kinds of distributive laws, including mixed distributive laws
[
bohm20112
,
power2002combining
]
and iterated distributive laws
[
cheng2011iterated
]
, are instances of this notion of monad.
An overview of the different kinds of monads internal to various bicategories can be found in Table
1
.
As such, the formal theory of monads provides a general setting to study various kinds of monads.

Bicategory

Notion of monad

Symmetric monoidal categories

Symmetric monoidal monad

Actegories
[
capucci2022actegories
]

Strong monad

Enriched categories
[
kelly1982basic
]

Enriched monad

Bicategory of monads

Distributive law

Table 1.
Various notions of monads

Foundations

In this paper, we work in
univalent foundations

[
hottbook
]
.
Univalent foundations is an extension of intensional type theory with the univalence axiom.
Roughly speaking, this axiom says that equivalent types are equal.
More precisely, we have a map
\mathsf{idtoequiv}
that sends identities
X=Y
to equivalences from
X
to
Y
, and the univalence axiom states that
\mathsf{idtoequiv}
is an equivalence itself.
This axiom has numerous effects on the mathematics developed in this foundation.
One consequence is function extensionality and another is that identity types must necessarily be proof relevant: since there could be multiple equivalence between two types, we could have different proofs of their equality.

In addition, the notion of category studied in this setting is that of
univalent categories

[
rezk_completion
]
.
In every category
\mathsf{C}
, we have a map
\mathsf{idtoiso}_{x,y}:x=y\rightarrow x\cong y
, and
\mathsf{C}
is
univalent
if
\mathsf{idtoiso}_{x,y}
is an equivalence for all
x,y:\mathsf{C}
.
From the univalence axiom, one can deduce that the category of sets is univalent.
The reason why this notion is interesting, is because in the set-theoretical semantics
[
simpset
]
, univalent categories correspond to ordinary categories.
Furthermore, every property expressible in type theory about univalent categories is closed under equivalence.

However, there are some challenges when working with univalent categories.
For instance, given a monad
m
on a category
\mathsf{C}
, we usually define the Kleisli category
\mathcal{K}(m)
of
m
to be the category whose objects are objects of
\mathsf{C}
and whose morphisms from
x
to
y
are morphisms
x\rightarrow m(x)

[
mac2013categories
]
.
Even if we assume
\mathsf{C}
to be univalent, the category
\mathcal{K}(m)
does not have to be univalent as well,
and this means that
\mathcal{K}(m)
does not give the Kleisli category for a monad on a univalent category.
A solution to this problem has already been given: instead of using
\mathcal{K}(m)
, we need to use its Rezk completion
[
univalence-principle
]
.
However, the necessary theorems about the Kleisli category (e.g., every monad gives rise to an adjunction via the Kleisli category) have not been proven in that work.

In the present paper, we study and formalize the formal theory of monads by Street
[
street1972formal
]
in univalent foundations.
More specifically, we formalize the key notions, which are the bicategory of monads and Eilenberg-Moore objects, and we illustrate them with numerous examples.
We also prove the two main theorems that relate monads to adjunctions: every adjunction gives rise to a monad and in a bicategory with Eilenberg-Moore objects, every monad gives rise to an adjunction.
In addition, we instantiate the formal theory of monads to deduce the main theorems about Kleisli categories.
The contribution of this paper is the development of the formal theory of monads in univalent foundations and a proof that in univalent foundations, every monad gives rise to an adjunction via the Kleisli category.

The abstract setting provided by the formal theory of monads is beneficial for formalization, which is our main motivation for this work.
The main theorems are only proven once in this setting, and afterwards they are instantiated to the relevant cases of interest without the need of reproving anything.
In addition, we think that this work would be useful to formalize the categorical semantics of linear logic
[
mellies2009categorical
]
or the enriched effect calculus
[
EggerMS14
]
.

Univalence also aids our development and it allows us to make several proof simpler and more elegant.
More specifically, univalence allows us to do induction on equivalences: to prove some statement for all adjoint equivalences,
it suffices to prove it only for the identity.
This idea is used in
,
where we characterize adjoint equivalences in the bicategory of monads.
In addition, certain types become mere propositions in locally univalent bicategories,
such as the type that says that an Eilenberg-Moore cone or a Kleisli object is universal (
and
) or that a 1-cell is monadic (
).
We use these observations in
and
to prove the equivalence of different formulations.

Formalization

The results in this paper are formalized using the Coq proof assistant
[
Coq:manual
]
, and they are integrated in the
UniMath
library
[
UniMath
]
.

UniMath
is under constant development, and the paper refers to the version with
git
hash 1054856.
The formalization consists of around 12,000 lines of code.
More specifically, the tool
coqwc
gives the following count:

Ψspec proof comments
Ψ4470 7839 182 total

In our development, displayed bicategories play a fundamental role.
We use them in
to give a modular proof that the bicategory of monads is univalent,
and in
to construct the pseudofunctor that sends every object
x
in a bicategory to the identity monad on
x
.

The main difficulty in this development arises from the coherences that have to be proven.
Since we use bicategories rather than 2-categories, it is necessary to decorate our terms with associators and unitors.
For instance, each of the monad laws in
contains one associator or one unitor.
A more extreme example is given by the composition of monads if we have a distributive law between them (
)
for which the multiplication uses four associators.
If we would have used 2-categories instead, there was no need to write down these associators and unitors.
As a consequence, whenever we prove a coherence, our terms are polluted with numerous associators and unitors.
While we essentially prove each coherence in the same way as one would for strict 2-categories,
the main difference is the increased amount of bureaucracy necessary to deal with the extra associators and unitors that are present in terms.

Each numbered environment in this paper comes with a link pointing to the relevant identifier in the formalization.
For example,
bicat
refers to the definition of a bicategory.
In the same repository
1
1

1

https://github.com/nmvdw/FormalTheoryOfMonadsUnivalently/tree/main/alectryon
, there is a version of the documentation compiled with Alectryon
[
Alectryon+SLE2020
]
.

Related work

The formal theory of monads was originally developed by Street
[
street1972formal
]
, and later extended by Lack and Street
[
lack2002formal
]
.
There are two differences between our work and Street’s work.
First of all, Street used strict 2-categories while we use bicategories.
As has already been noticed by Lack
[
lack2004composing
]
, this difference is rather minor.
The resulting definitions are similar: the only difference is that associators and unitors have to be put on the right places.
More fundamental is the second difference: we work in univalent foundations and we use univalent (bi)categories whereas Street works in set-theoretic foundations.
This affects the development in several ways.
While both bicategories and strict 2-categories have been defined and studied in a univalent setting
[
bicatspaper
]
, a coherence theorem
[
maclane1985coherence
,
power1989coherence
]
has not been proven in this setting.
In addition, since we work in an intensional setting, working with a strict 2-category is not significantly more convenient than working with a bicategory.
The reason for that is that equality proofs of associativity and unitality are present in terms to guarantee that the whole expression is well-typed.
As such, a coherence theorem would only have limited usability in our setting compared to a classical one.
Another difference is that in our framework, the usual definition of the Kleisli category does not give rise to a univalent category, and we need to work with its Rezk completion instead.
An overview of the main notions in bicategory theory can be found in various sources
[
10.1007/BFb0074299
,
johnson20212
,
lack20102
,
leinster:basic-bicats
]
.

Several formalizations have results about bicategory theory.
The coherence theorem
[
leinster:basic-bicats
]
is formalized in both Isabelle
[
nipkow2002isabelle
,
Stark20
]
and Lean
[
X20
]
, but neither of those are based on univalent foundations.
Some notions in bicategory theory have been formalized in Agda
[
norell2009dependently
]
, namely in the 1Lab
[
1lab
]
and the Agda-categories library
[
HuC21
]
.
However, neither of these cover the formal theory of monads.
We use
UniMath

[
UniMath
]
and its formalization of bicategories
[
bicatspaper
,
VvdW
]
.
Formalizations on category theory are more plentiful, and an overview can be found in
[
HuC21
]
.
Within the framework of univalent foundations, there is the HoTT library
[
BauerGLSSS17
,
GrossCS14
]
, Agda-UniMath
[
Agda-UniMath
]
, and Cubical Agda
[
VezzosiM019
]
.
Ahrens, Matthes, and Mörtberg formalized monads of categories in
UniMath

[
UniMath
]
.
They also defined a notion of signature, that allows for binding, and they showed that every signature gives rise to a monad
[
AhrensMM19
,
AhrensMM22
]
.

Overview

We start this paper by recalling some preliminary notions in
Section

2
.
Next we construct in
the bicategory of monads internal to bicategories and we prove that it is univalent.
We illustrate the material of
with various examples in
.
In
we discuss Eilenberg-Moore objects.
We follow that up in
by using Kleisli categories to construct Eilenberg-Moore objects in the opposite bicategory.
In
and
we prove some theorems in this setting.
We prove in
that every adjunction gives rise to a monad and that every monad gives rise to an adjunction under mild assumptions.
In
we define the notion of monadic adjunctions in an arbitrary bicategory and we characterize those using monadic adjunctions in categories.
We conclude in
.

2.
Preliminaries

In this section, we briefly recall some of the basic notions needed in this paper.
First of all, we use the notions of
propositions
and
sets
from univalent foundations.
Types
A
for which we have
x=y
for all
x,y:A
, are called
propositions
, and types
A
for which every
x=y
is a proposition, are called
sets
.
In addition, we assume that our foundation supports the
propositional truncation
: the truncation
\mathopen{}\left\|A\right\|\mathclose{}
is
A
with all its elements identified.
More concretely, we have a map
A\rightarrow\mathopen{}\left\|A\right\|\mathclose{}
and for all
x,y:\mathopen{}\left\|A\right\|\mathclose{}
, we have
x=y
.
Next we discuss some notions from bicategory theory
[
bicatspaper
,
10.1007/BFb0074299
,
leinster:basic-bicats
]
, and we start with
bicategories
.

{defi}

[
bicat
]

A
bicategory

\mathsf{B}
consists of a type
\mathsf{B}
of objects, for every
x,y:\mathsf{B}
a type
x\rightarrow y
of 1-cells, and for every
f,g:x\rightarrow y
, a
set

f\Rightarrow g
.
On this data, we have the following operations.

•

For every
x:\mathsf{B}
, a 1-cell
\operatorname{id}_{x}:x\rightarrow x
;

•

for all 1-cells
f:x\rightarrow y
and
g:y\rightarrow z
, a 1-cell
f\mathbin{\cdot}g:x\rightarrow z
;

•

for every 1-cell
f:x\rightarrow y
, a 2-cell
\operatorname{id}_{f}:f\Rightarrow f
;

•

for all 2-cells
\theta:f\Rightarrow g
and
\tau:g\Rightarrow h
, a 2-cell
\theta\bullet\tau:f\Rightarrow h
;

•

for every 1-cell
f:x\rightarrow y
, invertible 2-cells
\lambda_{f}:\operatorname{id}_{x}\mathbin{\cdot}f\Rightarrow f
and
\rho_{f}:f\mathbin{\cdot}\operatorname{id}_{y}\Rightarrow f
;

•

for all 1-cells
f:w\rightarrow x
,
g:x\rightarrow y
, and
h:y\rightarrow z
, an invertible 2-cell

\alpha_{f,g,h}:f\mathbin{\cdot}(g\mathbin{\cdot}h)\Rightarrow(f\mathbin{\cdot}g)\mathbin{\cdot}h
.

If the relevant 1-cells are clear from the context, we write
\lambda
,
\rho
, and
\alpha
instead of
\lambda_{f}
,
\rho_{f}
, and
\alpha_{f,g,h}
respectively.
We can also whisker 2-cells with 1-cells in two ways.
Given 1-cells and 2-cells as depicted in the diagram on the left below, we have a 2-cell
f\vartriangleleft\tau:f\mathbin{\cdot}g_{1}\Rightarrow f\mathbin{\cdot}g_{2}
, and from 1-cells and 2-cells as depicted in the diagram on the right below, we get a 2-cell
\tau\vartriangleright g:f_{1}\mathbin{\cdot}g\Rightarrow f_{2}\mathbin{\cdot}g
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces x}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces z}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau}

The laws that need to be satisfied, can be found in the literature
[
bicatspaper
, Definition 2.1]
.

Note that we use diagrammatic order for composition instead of compositional order.
We use the notation
\underline{\mathsf{B}(x,y)}
for the category whose objects are 1-cells
f:x\rightarrow y
and whose morphisms from
f:x\rightarrow y
to
g:x\rightarrow y
are 2-cells
\tau:f\Rightarrow g
.
Given a 1-cell
f:x\rightarrow y
and an object
w:\mathsf{B}
, we have a functor
(-\mathbin{\cdot}f)_{w}:\underline{\mathsf{B}(w,x)}\rightarrow\underline{\mathsf{B}(w,y)}
, which sends a 1-cell
g:w\rightarrow x
to
g\mathbin{\cdot}f
and a 2-cell
\tau:g_{1}\Rightarrow g_{2}
to
\tau\vartriangleright f
.

The core example of a bicategory in this paper is
\mathsf{UnivCat}
.
Its objects are
univalent
categories, the 1-cells are functors, and the 2-cells are natural transformations.
We also have a bicategory
\mathsf{Cat}
whose objects are (not necessarily univalent) categories, 1-cells are functors, and 2-cells are natural transformations.

In this paper, we also make use of
univalent
bicategories.
To define this property, we use that between every two objects
x,y:\mathsf{B}
, we have a type
x\simeq y
of
adjoint equivalences
between them.
In addition, for all 1-cells
f,g:x\rightarrow y
, there is a type
f\cong g
of
invertible 2-cells
between them.
For the precise definition of these notions, we refer the reader to the literature
[
bicatspaper
, Definitions 2.4 and 2.5]
.

{defi}

Let
\mathsf{B}
be a bicategory.

•

(
is_univalent_2_1
) Using path induction, we define a function
\mathsf{idtoiso}^{2,1}_{f,g}:f=g\rightarrow f\cong g
for all 1-cells
f,g:x\rightarrow y
. The bicategory
\mathsf{B}
is
locally univalent
if
\mathsf{idtoiso}^{2,1}_{f,g}
is an equivalence for all
f
and
g
.

•

(
is_univalent_2_0
) Using path induction, we define a function
\mathsf{idtoiso}^{2,0}_{x,y}:x=y\rightarrow x\simeq y
for all objects
x
and
y
. We say that
\mathsf{B}
is
globally univalent
if
\mathsf{idtoiso}^{2,0}_{x,y}
is an equivalence for all
x
and
y
.

•

(
is_univalent_2
) The bicategory
\mathsf{B}
is
univalent
if it is both locally and globally univalent.

The bicategory
\mathsf{UnivCat}
of univalent categories is both locally and globally univalent.
However,
\mathsf{Cat}
, whose objects are not required to be univalent, is neither.

In a univalent bicategory, we can do induction on adjoint equivalences and invertible 2-cells.
This is similar to the J-rule for propositional equality,
which says that to prove some property for every
p:x=y
,
it suffices to show this property for all reflexivity paths.
For adjoint equivalences and invertible 2-cells, we state induction as follows.

Proposition 1
.

Let
\mathsf{B}
be a bicategory.

•

(
J_2_1
)
Suppose, that
\mathsf{B}
is locally univalent.
Let
P
be a type family on the invertible 2-cells of
\mathsf{B}
.
More specifically,
P
is a type depending on objects
x,y:\mathsf{B}
, 1-cells
f,g:x\rightarrow y
, and invertible 2-cells
f\cong g
.
Furthermore, we assume that we have for every
f:x\rightarrow y
an inhabitant of
P(\operatorname{id}_{f})
.
Then we have an inhabitant of
P(\tau)
for every invertible 2-cell
\tau:f\cong g
.

•

(
J_2_0
)
Suppose, that
\mathsf{B}
is globally univalent.
Let
P
be a type family on the adjoint equivalences of
\mathsf{B}
.
More specifically,
P
is a type depending on objects
x,y:\mathsf{B}
and adjoint equivalences 1-cells
f:x\simeq y
.
Furthermore, we assume that we have for every object
x
an inhabitant of
P(\operatorname{id}_{x})
.
Then we have an inhabitant of
P(f)
for every adjoint equivalence
f:x\simeq y
.

If we have a bicategory
\mathsf{B}
, then we define the bicategory
\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{op}}
by ‘reversing the 1-cells’ in
\mathsf{B}
.
More precisely, objects are the same as objects in
\mathsf{B}
, 1-cells from
x
to
y
in
\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{op}}
are 1-cells
y\rightarrow x
in
\mathsf{B}
, while 2-cells from
f:y\rightarrow x
to
g:y\rightarrow x
are 2-cells
f\Rightarrow g
in
\mathsf{B}
.
In addition, from a bicategory
\mathsf{B}
, we obtain
\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{co}}
by ‘reversing the 2-cells’.
Objects and 1-cells in
\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{co}}
are the same as objects and 1-cells in
\mathsf{B}
respectively, but a 2-cell from
f
to
g
in
\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{co}}
is a 2-cell
g\Rightarrow f
in
\mathsf{B}
.
Next we define
pseudofunctors
.

{defi}

[
psfunctor
]

Let
\mathsf{B}_{1}
and
\mathsf{B}_{2}
be bicategories.
A
pseudofunctor

F:\mathsf{B}_{1}\rightarrow\mathsf{B}_{2}
consists of

•

A function
F:\mathsf{B}_{1}\rightarrow\mathsf{B}_{2}
;

•

For every
x,y:\mathsf{B}_{1}
a function that maps
f:x\rightarrow y
to
F(f):F\>x\rightarrow F\>y
;

•

For all 1-cell
f,g:x\rightarrow y
a function that maps
\theta:f\Rightarrow g
to
F(\theta):F(f)\Rightarrow F(g)
;

•

For every
x:\mathsf{B}_{1}
, an invertible 2-cell
F_{i}(x):\operatorname{id}_{F(x)}\Rightarrow F(\operatorname{id}_{x})
;

•

For all
f:x\rightarrow y
and
g:y\rightarrow z
, an invertible 2-cell
F_{c}(f,g):F(f)\mathbin{\cdot}F(g)\Rightarrow F(f\mathbin{\cdot}g)
.

The coherences that need to be satisfied, can be found in the literature
[
bicatspaper
, Definition 2.12]
.

In applications, we are interested in a wide variety of bicategories beside
\mathsf{UnivCat}
, and among those are the bicategory
\mathsf{UnivCat}_{\mathsf{Terminal}}
of categories with a terminal object
and the bicategory
\mathsf{SymMonUnivCat}
of symmetric monoidal categories.
These examples have something in common: their objects are categories equipped with some extra structure, the 1-cells are structure preserving functors, and the 2-cells are structure preserving natural transformations.
We capture this pattern using
displayed bicategories

[
bicatspaper
, Definition 6.1]
.
This notion is an adaptation of
displayed categories

[
AhrensL19
]
to the bicategorical setting.

To get an idea of what displayed bicategories are, let us first briefly discuss displayed categories.
A displayed category
\mathsf{D}
over
\mathsf{C}
represents structure and properties to be added to the objects and morphisms of
\mathsf{C}
.
For every object
x:\mathsf{C}
, we have a type of
displayed objects

\mathsf{D}_{x}
and for every morphism
f:x\rightarrow y
and displayed objects
\overline{x}:\mathsf{D}_{x}
and
\overline{y}:\mathsf{D}_{y}
, we have a set
\overline{x}\xrightarrow{f}\overline{y}
of
displayed morphisms
.
For example, if for
\mathsf{C}
we take the category of sets, an example of a displayed category would be group structures.
The displayed objects over a set
X
are group structures over
X
, while the displayed morphisms over
f:X\rightarrow Y
between two group structures are proofs that
f
preserves the group operations.
Every displayed category
\mathsf{D}
gives rise to the
total category

\int\mathsf{D}
and a functor
\pi_{\mathsf{D}}:\int\mathsf{D}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}
.
In the example we mentioned before,
\int\mathsf{D}
would be the category of groups and
\pi_{\mathsf{D}}
maps a group to its underlying set.

In the bicategorical setting, we use a similar approach, but a displayed bicategory should not only have displayed objects and 1-cells, but also displayed 2-cells.
More precisely, we define displayed bicategories as follows.

{defi}

[
disp_bicat
]

A
displayed bicategory

\mathsf{D}
over a bicategory
\mathsf{B}
consists of the following data.

•

A type
{\mathsf{D}}_{x}
of
displayed objects
for every
x:\mathsf{B}
;

•

a type
\overline{x}\xrightarrow{f}\overline{y}
of
displayed morphisms
for all 1-cells
f:x\rightarrow y
and displayed objects
\overline{x}:{\mathsf{D}}_{x}
and
\overline{y}:{\mathsf{D}}_{y}
;

•

a set
\overline{f}\xRightarrow{\theta}\overline{g}
of
displayed 2-cells
for all 2-cells
\theta:f\Rightarrow g
and displayed morphisms
\overline{f}:\overline{x}\xrightarrow{f}\overline{y}
and
\overline{g}:\overline{x}\xrightarrow{g}\overline{y}
.

In addition, there are displayed versions of every operation of bicategories.
For example, for every
x:\mathsf{B}
and
\overline{x}:{\mathsf{D}}_{x}
, we have the displayed identity
\overline{\operatorname{id}_{x}}:\overline{x}\xrightarrow{\operatorname{id}_{x}}\overline{x}
, and from two displayed morphisms
\overline{f}:\overline{x}\xrightarrow{f}\overline{y}
and
\overline{g}:\overline{y}\xrightarrow{g}\overline{z}
we get a displayed morphism
\overline{f}\mathbin{\cdot}\overline{g}:\overline{x}\xrightarrow{f\mathbin{\cdot}g}\overline{z}
.
A list of the operations and laws can be found in the literature
[
bicatspaper
, Definition 6.1]
.

Just like for displayed categories, every displayed bicategory
\mathsf{D}
gives rise to bicategory
\int\mathsf{D}
, called the
total bicategory
, and a pseudofunctor
\pi_{\mathsf{D}}:\int\mathsf{D}\rightarrow\mathsf{B}
.

Problem 2.1
.

Given a displayed bicategory
\mathsf{D}
over
\mathsf{B}
, to construct a bicategory
\int\mathsf{D}
and a pseudofunctor
\pi_{\mathsf{D}}:\int\mathsf{D}\rightarrow\mathsf{B}
.

Construction 2

(for Problem
2.1
;
total_bicat
)
.

The bicategory
\int\mathsf{D}
is defined as follows.

•

Its objects are pairs
x:\mathsf{B}
together with
\overline{x}:{\mathsf{D}}_{x}
;

•

its 1-cells from
(x,\overline{x})
to
(y,\overline{y})
are pairs
f:x\rightarrow y
together with
\overline{f}:\overline{x}\xrightarrow{f}\overline{y}
;

•

its 2-cells from
(f,\overline{f})
to
(g,\overline{g})
are pairs
\tau:f\Rightarrow g
together with
\overline{\tau}:\overline{f}\xRightarrow{\tau}\overline{g}
.

The pseudofunctor
\pi_{\mathsf{D}}
sends objects
(x,\overline{x})
to
x
, 1-cells
(f,\overline{f})
to
f
, and 2-cells
(\theta,\overline{\theta})
to
\theta
.
∎

Displayed bicategories are useful to construct univalent bicategories in a modular way.
For this, we need
univalent displayed bicategories

[
bicatspaper
, Definition 7.3]
.
Displayed univalence is defined in a similar way as univalence for bicategories.
Concretely, we use the notion of
displayed invertible 2-cells

[
bicatspaper
, Definition 7.1]

and of
displayed invertible 2-cells

[
bicatspaper
, Definition 7.2]
.
The type of displayed invertible 2-cells over an invertible 2-cell
\tau:f\cong g
is denoted by
\overline{f}\cong_{\tau}\overline{g}

for displayed 1-cells
\overline{f}:\overline{x}\xrightarrow{f}\overline{y}
and
\overline{g}:\overline{x}\xrightarrow{g}\overline{y}
.
The type of displayed adjoint equivalences over an adjoint equivalence
f:x\simeq y
is denoted by
\overline{x}\simeq_{f}\overline{y}

for displayed objects
\overline{x}:{\mathsf{D}}_{x}
and
\overline{y}:{\mathsf{D}}_{y}
.

{defi}

Let
\mathsf{B}
be a bicategory and let
\mathsf{D}
be a displayed bicategory over
\mathsf{B}
.

•

(
disp_univalent_2_1
) Given a path
p:f=g
,
we define a function
\overline{\mathsf{idtoiso}}^{2,1}_{p}:\overline{f}=_{p}\overline{g}\rightarrow\overline{f}\cong_{\mathsf{idtoiso}^{2,1}_{f,g}\>p}\overline{g}
using path induction
for all 1-cells
\overline{f}:\overline{x}\xrightarrow{f}\overline{y}
and
\overline{g}:\overline{x}\xrightarrow{g}\overline{y}
.
We say that
\mathsf{D}
is
locally univalent
if
\mathsf{idtoiso}^{2,1}_{\overline{f},\overline{g}}
is an equivalence
for all displayed 1-cells
\overline{f}:\overline{x}\xrightarrow{f}\overline{y}
and
\overline{g}:\overline{x}\xrightarrow{g}\overline{y}
, and paths
p:f=g
.

•

(
disp_univalent_2_0
) Given a path
p:x=y
,
we define a
\overline{\mathsf{idtoiso}}^{2,0}_{p}:\overline{x}=_{p}\overline{y}\rightarrow\overline{x}\simeq_{\mathsf{idtoiso}^{2,0}_{x,y}(p)}\overline{y}
using path induction
for all objects
\overline{x}:{\mathsf{D}}_{x}
and
\overline{y}:{\mathsf{D}}_{y}
.
We say that
\mathsf{D}
is
globally univalent
if
\mathsf{idtoiso}^{2,0}_{\overline{x},\overline{y}}
is an equivalence
for all objects
\overline{x}:{\mathsf{D}}_{x}
and
\overline{y}:{\mathsf{D}}_{y}
and paths
p:x=y
.

•

(
disp_univalent_2
) The displayed bicategory
\mathsf{D}
is
univalent
if it is both locally and globally univalent.

If we have a displayed bicategory
\mathsf{D}
over
\mathsf{B}
and if both
\mathsf{B}
and
\mathsf{D}
are univalent, then
\int\mathsf{D}
is univalent as well
[
bicatspaper
, Theorem 7.4]
.

In numerous different examples, we look at displayed bicategories whose displayed 2-cells are actually trivial in a certain sense.
More precisely, we look at two properties.
One of them expresses that between all displayed 1-cells
\overline{f}:\overline{x}\xrightarrow{f}\overline{y}
and
\overline{g}:\overline{x}\xrightarrow{g}\overline{y}
, there is at most one displayed 2-cell.
A displayed bicategory satisfying that property, is called a
local preorder
: it expresses that the type
\overline{f}\xRightarrow{\tau}\overline{g}
is a proposition.
The other property is
locally groupoidal
, and it says that every displayed 2-cell over an invertible 2-cell is again invertible.
One way to construct such displayed bicategories, is as follows.

{defi}

[
disp_cell_unit_bicat
]

Suppose, we have a bicategory
\mathsf{B}
and

•

for each
x:\mathsf{B}
a type
\mathsf{D}_{x}
;

•

for every 1-cell
f:x\rightarrow y
and elements
\overline{x}:\mathsf{D}_{x}
and
\overline{y}:\mathsf{D}_{y}
a type
\mathsf{D}_{\overline{x},\overline{y},f}
;

•

for every
x:\mathsf{B}
and
\overline{x}:\mathsf{D}_{x}
an inhabitant of
\mathsf{D}_{\overline{x},\overline{x},\operatorname{id}_{x}}
;

•

for all
\overline{f}:\mathsf{D}_{\overline{x},\overline{y},f}
and
\overline{g}:\mathsf{D}_{\overline{y},\overline{z},g}
an inhabitant of
\mathsf{D}_{\overline{x},\overline{z},f\mathbin{\cdot}g}
.

Then we get a displayed bicategory over
\mathsf{B}
whose type of 2-cells between every pair of 1-cells is defined to be the unit type.

Note that every displayed bicategory constructed using
Section

2
is both a local preorder and locally groupoidal.
To illustrate the notion of displayed bicategory, let us look at some examples.
These were already considered in previous work
[
ahrens2022semantics
,
wullaert2022univalent
]
.

{exa}

[
univ_cat_with_terminal_obj
]

Using
Section

2
, we define a displayed bicategory
\mathsf{d}\mathsf{UnivCat}_{\mathsf{Terminal}}
over
\mathsf{UnivCat}
.

•

Objects over
\mathsf{C}
are terminal objects
T_{\mathsf{C}}
in
\mathsf{C}
.

•

Displayed 1-cells over
F:\mathsf{C}_{1}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}_{2}
are proofs that
F
preserves terminal objects.

We define
\mathsf{UnivCat}_{\mathsf{Terminal}}
to be
\int\mathsf{d}\mathsf{UnivCat}_{\mathsf{Terminal}}
.

{exa}

[
disp_bicat_univmon
]

We define a displayed bicategory
\mathsf{dMonUnivCat}
over
\mathsf{UnivCat}
as follows.

•

Objects over
\mathsf{C}
are monoidal structures over
\mathsf{C}
;

•

1-cells over
F:\mathsf{C}_{1}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}_{2}
are structures that
F
is a lax monoidal functor;

•

2-cells over
\theta:F\Rightarrow G
are proofs that
\theta
is a monoidal transformation.

We denote its total bicategory by
\mathsf{MonUnivCat}
.

Note that similarly, one can define a displayed bicategory
\mathsf{dSymMonUnivCat}
over
\mathsf{UnivCat}
whose total bicategory
\mathsf{SymMonUnivCat}
is the bicategory of symmetric monoidal categories.
In the remainder of this paper, we use several operations on displayed bicategories.

{defi}

We have the following operations on displayed bicategories:

(1)

(
disp_dirprod_bicat
) Given displayed bicategories
\mathsf{D}
and
\mathsf{D}^{\prime}
over
\mathsf{B}
, we construct a displayed bicategory
\mathsf{D}\times\mathsf{D}^{\prime}
over
\mathsf{B}
whose objects, 1-cells and 2-cells are pairs of objects, 1-cells, and 2-cells in
\mathsf{D}
and
\mathsf{D}^{\prime}
respectively.

(2)

(
sigma_bicat
) Suppose that we have a displayed bicategory
\mathsf{D}
over
\mathsf{B}
and a displayed bicategory
\mathsf{D}^{\prime}
over
\int\mathsf{D}
.
We construct a displayed bicategory
\sum(\mathsf{D}^{\prime})
over
\mathsf{B}
by setting the objects over
x
to be pairs of
(\overline{x},\overline{\overline{x}})
of objects
\overline{x}:{D}_{x}
and
\overline{\overline{x}}:{\mathsf{D}^{\prime}}_{(x,\overline{x})}
.

(3)

(
disp_fullsubbicat
) Let
\mathsf{B}
be a bicategory and let
P
be a predicate on the objects of
\mathsf{B}
.
We define a displayed bicategory
\mathsf{dFullSub}(P)
over
\mathsf{B}
by setting the displayed objects over
x
to be proofs of
P(x)
and the types of 1-cells between every pair of objects and of 2-cells between every pair of 1-cells are defined to be the unit type.

The last notion we discuss, is the notion of a
section
of a displayed bicategory.
Intuitively, a section assigns to every
x
a displayed object
\overline{x}:\mathsf{D}_{x}
in a pseudofunctorial way.
As such every section
s
of
\mathsf{D}
induces a pseudofunctor

3.
s

:
\mathsf{B}
→∫D
suchthatevery
x :
\mathsf{B}

is\emph{definitionally}equalto
π
_
\mathsf{D}
(

4.
s

(x))
.\par\defi[\hrefhttps://nmvdw.github.io/FormalTheoryOfMonadsUnivalently/UniMath.Bicategories.DisplayedBicats.DispBicatSection.html#section_disp_bicat]A\textbf{section}$s$of$\mathsf{D}$consistsofthefollowingdata.\begin{itemize}\itemize@item@For every object $x:\mathsf{B}$ a displayed object ${s}_{x}:{\mathsf{D}}_{x}$;
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@for every 1-cell $f:x\rightarrow y$ a displayed 1-cell ${s}_{f}:{s}_{x}\xrightarrow{f}{s}_{y}$;
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@for every 2-cell $\theta:f\Rightarrow g$ a displayed 2-cell ${s}_{\theta}:{s}_{f}\xRightarrow{\theta}{s}_{g}$;
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@for every $x:\mathsf{B}$ an invertible 2-cell ${s}_{i}(x):\overline{\operatorname{id}_{{s}_{x}}}\xRightarrow{\operatorname{id}_{\operatorname{id}_{x}}}{s}_{\operatorname{id}_{x}}$;
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@for every $f:x\rightarrow y$ and $g:y\rightarrow z$ an invertible 2-cell ${s}_{c}(f,g):{s}_{f}\mathbin{\cdot}{s}_{g}\xRightarrow{\operatorname{id}_{f\mathbin{\cdot}g}}{s}_{f\mathbin{\cdot}g}$.
\end{itemize}Wealsorequireseveralcoherencesreminiscentofthelawsofpseudofunctors,andforaprecisedescriptionofthose,wereferthereadertotheformalization.\par\begin{problem}Given a displayed bicategory $\mathsf{D}$ on $\mathsf{B}$ and a section $s$ on $\mathsf{D}$, to construct a pseudofunctor $\par\@@numbered@section{section}{toc}{s} : $\mathsf{B}$ \rightarrow\int D$.\end{problem}\par\begin{constrInternal}We define the pseudofunctor $\par\@@numbered@section{section}{toc}{s}$asfollows:itsendsobjects$x$tothepair$(x, {s}_{x})$,1-cells$f$to$(f, {s}_{f})$,and2-cells$\tau: f \Rightarrow g$to$(\tau, {s}_{\tau})$.\qed\end{constrInternal}\par\par\@@numbered@section{section}{toc}{The Bicategory of Monads}
Two concepts play a key role in the formal theory of monads: the bicategory of monads $\mathsf{Mnd}(\mathsf{B})$ internal to $\mathsf{B}$ and Eilenberg-Moore objects.
In this section, we study the first concept, and we use displayed bicategories to construct the bicategory $\mathsf{Mnd}(\mathsf{B})$ from a bicategory $\mathsf{B}$.
We also show that $\mathsf{Mnd}(\mathsf{B})$ must be univalent if $\mathsf{B}$ is.
\par The main idea behind the construction is to split up monads in several independent parts.
We first define $\mathsf{dEndo}(\mathsf{B})$ whose displayed objects over $x$ are 1-cells $e:x\rightarrow x$.
After that, we define $\mathsf{dUnit}(\mathsf{B})$ and $\mathsf{dMult}(\mathsf{B})$ whose displayed objects are the unit and multiplication of the monad respectively.
We finally take a full subcategory for the monad laws.
A pictorial summary can be found in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{fig:construction}.

Let $\mathsf{B}$ be a bicategory.
Define a displayed bicategory $\mathsf{dEndo}(\mathsf{B})$ over $\mathsf{B}$ as follows.
\begin{itemize}\itemize@item@The displayed objects over an object $x$ are 1-cells $e_{x}:x\rightarrow x$;
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@the displayed 1-cells over a 1-cell $f:x\rightarrow y$ from $e_{x}:x\rightarrow x$ to $e_{y}:y\rightarrow y$ are 2-cells $\theta_{f}:f\cdot e_{y}\Rightarrow e_{x}\cdot f$;

\par Next we define two displayed bicategories over $\mathsf{Endo}(\mathsf{B})$.
For both, we use \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{def:dispcat-to-dispbicat}.
\par\defi[\hrefhttps://nmvdw.github.io/FormalTheoryOfMonadsUnivalently/UniMath.Bicategories.DisplayedBicats.Examples.MonadsLax.html#disp_add_unit]

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}$$\end{itemize}\par Next we define $\mathsf{dMndData}(\mathsf{B})$ to be $\sum(\mathsf{dUnit}(\mathsf{B})\times\mathsf{dMult}(\mathsf{B}))$ and we denote its total bicategory by $\mathsf{MndData}(\mathsf{B})$.
To obtain $\mathsf{Mnd}(\mathsf{B})$, we take a full subbicategory.
\par\defi[\hrefhttps://nmvdw.github.io/FormalTheoryOfMonadsUnivalently/UniMath.Bicategories.DisplayedBicats.Examples.MonadsLax.html#disp_mnd]

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-1.78828pt}{30.37364pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}$$We define $\mathsf{dMnd}(\mathsf{B})$ to be $\sum(\mathsf{dFullSub}(\mathsf{isMnd}))$, and we denote its total bicategory by $\mathsf{Mnd}(\mathsf{B})$.
\par Note that the predicate $\mathsf{isMnd}$ contains all monad laws.
Alternatively, we could have defined a displayed bicategory for each monad law.
We refrained from doing so, because that would not further simplify the proof that $\mathsf{Mnd}(\mathsf{B})$ is univalent.
\par Before we continue, let us discuss the objects, 1-cells, and 2-cells of $\mathsf{Mnd}(\mathsf{B})$, and fix the relevant notation for the remainder of this paper.
This also explains what the displayed objects, 1-cells, and 2-cells in $\mathsf{dMnd}(\mathsf{B})$ are.
Monads $m$ in $\mathsf{B}$ are inhabitants of the $\sum$-type whose components are given by
\begin{itemize}\itemize@item@an object $\mathsf{ob}_{m}:\mathsf{B}$;
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@a 1-cell $\mathsf{mor}_{m}:\mathsf{ob}_{m}\rightarrow\mathsf{ob}_{m}$;
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@a 2-cell $\mathsf{\eta}_{m}:\operatorname{id}_{\mathsf{ob}_{m}}\Rightarrow m$;
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@a 2-cell $\mathsf{\mu}_{m}:m\mathbin{\cdot}m\Rightarrow m$.
\end{itemize}Note that this data must satisfy the laws given in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{def:mnd}.
If no confusion arises, we write $m$ instead of $\mathsf{mor}_{m}$.
\par Monad morphisms $f:m_{1}\rightarrow m_{2}$ between monads $m_{1}$ and $m_{2}$ are inhabitants of the $\sum$-type whose components consists of a 1-cell $\mathsf{mor}_{f}:\mathsf{ob}_{m_{1}}\rightarrow\mathsf{ob}_{m_{2}}$ and a 2-cell $\mathsf{cell}_{f}:f\mathbin{\cdot}m_{2}\Rightarrow m_{1}\mathbin{\cdot}f$,
such that this data satisfies the laws described in \lx@cref{creftypeplural\lx@tilde refnum}{def:unit} and\lx@nobreakspace\lx@cref{refnum}{def:mult}.
Note that there also are several components in this sigma type that are given by the unit type,
because we take a full subbicategory (\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{def:mnd}).
However, we can safely ignore those, because they do not add any information.
Lastly, the type of monad 2-cells $\gamma:f_{1}\Rightarrow f_{2}$ between monad morphisms is given by a $\sum$-type that contains a 2-cell $\mathsf{cell}_{\gamma}:f_{1}\Rightarrow f_{2}$.
In addition, 2-cells of monads satisfy the requirement given in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{def:endo}.
For 2-cells of monads there also are several components that are given by the unit type, which we added in \lx@cref{creftypeplural\lx@tilde refnum}{def:unit}, \lx@cref{refnum}{def:mult} and\lx@nobreakspace\lx@cref{refnum}{def:mnd},
and again we can safely ignore those.
We write $f$ instead of $\mathsf{mor}_{f}$ and $\gamma$ instead of $\mathsf{cell}_{\gamma}$ if no confusion arises.
\par In \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{bicatspaper}{}{}, Definition 6.7]}, another bicategory of monads internal to $\mathsf{B}$ is defined.
However, here we consider lax morphisms, while they only consider pseudomorphisms, i.e., morphisms $f:m_{1}\rightarrow m_{2}$ for which $\mathsf{cell}_{f}$ is invertible.
For the development of the formal theory of monads, lax morphisms are needed.
\par Next we look at the univalence of $\mathsf{Mnd}(\mathsf{B})$, and to prove it, we use displayed univalence.
More specifically, it suffices to prove the displayed univalence of $\mathsf{dEndo}(\mathsf{B})$, $\mathsf{dUnit}(\mathsf{B})$, and $\mathsf{dMult}(\mathsf{B})$.
We also need $\mathsf{B}$ to be univalent.
\par\begin{prop}If $\mathsf{B}$ is univalent, then so is $\mathsf{Mnd}(\mathsf{B})$.
\end{prop}\par\begin{proof}To establish the univalence of $\mathsf{Mnd}(\mathsf{B})$, it sufficies to show that each of $\mathsf{dEndo}(\mathsf{B})$, $\mathsf{dUnit}(\mathsf{B})$, $\mathsf{dMult}(\mathsf{B})$, and $\mathsf{dMnd}(\mathsf{B})$ is univalent.
For $\mathsf{dMnd}(\mathsf{B})$, $\mathsf{dUnit}(\mathsf{B})$, and $\mathsf{dMult}(\mathsf{B})$ one can use more general statements from the literature.
For $\mathsf{dEndo}(\mathsf{B})$ one uses the same techniques as in the literature \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{bicatspaper}{}{}, Theorem 9.10]}.
\end{proof}\par We also characterize invertible 2-cells and adjoint equivalences in $\mathsf{Mnd}(\mathsf{B})$.
For the invertible 2-cells, we can take a direct approach and we give a concrete definition of the inverse.
\par\begin{prop}A 2-cell $\gamma$ between monad morphisms is invertible, if the underlying 2-cell $\mathsf{cell}_{\gamma}:f_{1}\Rightarrow f_{2}$ is invertible.
\end{prop}\par\begin{proof}The underlying 2-cell of the inverse of $\gamma$ is given by $\mathsf{cell}_{\gamma}^{-1}$.
For the verification that this is indeed a 2-cell of monads, we refer the reader to the formalization.
\end{proof}\par Next we characterize adjoint equivalences in $\mathsf{Mnd}(\mathsf{B})$.
However, to do so, we do not use a direct approach, because the resulting construction is more complicated.
Instead, we assume that $\mathsf{B}$ is univalent, and we use equivalence induction (\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{prop:equiv-ind}).
\par\begin{prop}Let $\mathsf{B}$ be a univalent bicategory and let $f:m_{1}\rightarrow m_{2}$ be a 1-cell in $\mathsf{Mnd}(B)$.
If $\mathsf{mor}_{f}$ is an adjoint equivalence and $\mathsf{cell}_{f}$ is an invertible 2-cell, then $f$ is an adjoint equivalence.
\end{prop}\par\begin{proof}We use induction on adjoint equivalences.
This allows us to prove the following special case:
given an object $x:\mathsf{B}$ and two monad structures $m_{1}$ and $m_{2}$ over $x$,
then every displayed 1-cell $f$ over the identity $\operatorname{id}_{x}$ is an adjoint equivalence
whenever $\mathsf{cell}_{f}$ is an invertible 2-cell.
Note that the 2-cell $\mathsf{cell}_{f}$ goes from $m_{1}\cdot\operatorname{id}_{x}$ to $\operatorname{id}_{x}\cdot\,m_{2}$.
We demonstrate how the desired inverse $r:m_{2}\rightarrow m_{1}$ is constructed.
\begin{itemize}\itemize@item@We define $\mathsf{mor}_{r}$ to be $\operatorname{id}_{x}$;
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@we define $\mathsf{cell}_{r}$ to be the following composition.
$$\hbox to379.74pt{\vbox to20.44pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 189.86803pt\lower-7.88194pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

and one can show that $f$ and $r$ form an adjoint equivalence.
\end{proof}\par Equivalence induction simplifies this proof,
because it allows us to consider only the identity rather than arbitrary adjoint equivalences.
\par\par\@@numbered@section{section}{toc}{Examples of Monads}
Next we look at examples of monads, and we start by characterizing monads internal to several bicategories.
Let us start by observing that monads in the bicategory $\mathsf{UnivCat}$ of categories correspond to monads as how they usually are defined in category theory.
However, since this notion of monad is defined in \emph{every} bicategory, we can also look at other bicategories, such as $\mathsf{SymMonUnivCat}$,and $\mathsf{UnivCat}_{\mathsf{Terminal}}$.
\par In a wide variety of applications, one is interested in monads in a bicategory of categories with some extra structure.
For example, symmetric monoidal monads are monads internal to the bicategory of symmetric monoidal categories.
Strong monads are monads in the bicategory of left actegories.
The bicategories in these two examples can be constructed as a total bicategory of some displayed bicategory over $\mathsf{UnivCat}$.
To characterize monads in total bicategories, we define \emph{displayed monads}.
\par\defi[\hrefhttps://nmvdw.github.io/FormalTheoryOfMonadsUnivalently/UniMath.Bicategories.Monads.Examples.MonadsInTotalBicat.html#disp_mnd]
Let $\mathsf{B}$ be a bicategory and let $\mathsf{D}$ be a displayed bicategory over $\mathsf{B}$ and suppose that $\mathsf{D}$ is a local preorder.
A {displayed monad} $\overline{m}$ over a monad $m$ in $\mathsf{B}$ consists of
\begin{itemize}\itemize@item@a displayed object $\overline{\mathsf{ob}_{m}}:{\mathsf{D}}_{\mathsf{ob}_{m}}$;
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@a displayed 1-cell $\overline{\mathsf{mor}_{m}}:\overline{\mathsf{ob}_{m}}\xrightarrow{m}\overline{\mathsf{ob}_{m}}$;
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@a displayed 2-cell $\overline{\mathsf{\eta}_{m}}:\overline{\operatorname{id}_{\mathsf{ob}_{m}}}\xRightarrow{\mathsf{\eta}_{m}}\overline{m}$;
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@a displayed 2-cell $\overline{\mathsf{\mu}_{m}}:\overline{m}\mathbin{\cdot}\overline{m}\xRightarrow{\mathsf{\mu}_{m}}\overline{m}$.
\end{itemize}\par Note that we do not require any coherences in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{def:disp-mnd}.
Any coherence would hold vacuously, because the involved displayed bicategory is assumed to be a local preorder.
\par\begin{problem}Given a monad $m$ and a displayed monad $\overline{m}$ over $m$, to construct a monad $\int m$ in $\int D$.
\end{problem}\par\begin{constrInternal}We construct $\int m$ as follows.
\begin{itemize}\itemize@item@We define the object $\mathsf{ob}_{\int m}$ to be $(\mathsf{ob}_{m},\overline{\mathsf{ob}_{m}})$;
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@we define the 1-cell $\mathsf{mor}_{\int m}$ to be $(m,\overline{m})$;
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@we define the unit $\mathsf{\eta}_{\int m}$ to be $(\mathsf{\eta}_{m},\overline{\mathsf{\eta}_{m}})$;
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@we define multiplication $\mathsf{\mu}_{\int m}$ to be $(\mathsf{\mu}_{m},\overline{\mathsf{\mu}_{m}})$. \qed\end{itemize}\end{constrInternal}\par We can also show that every monad in $\int\mathsf{D}$ gives rise to a monad $m$ in $\mathsf{B}$ and a displayed monad over $m$.
The monad in $\mathsf{B}$ is obtained by taking the first projection and the displayed monad by taking the second projection.
\par\exa[\hrefhttps://nmvdw.github.io/FormalTheoryOfMonadsUnivalently/UniMath.Bicategories.Monads.Examples.MonadsInStructuredCategories.html#make_mnd_univ_cat_with_terminal_obj]
Given a monad $m$ of categories,
a displayed monad in $\mathsf{d}\mathsf{UnivCat}_{\mathsf{Terminal}}$ over $m$ consists of a terminal object in $\mathsf{ob}_{m}$ and a proof that $m$ preserves terminal objects.
\par Analogously, we can characterize monads in $\mathsf{SymMonUnivCat}$.
Next we look at monads in $\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{op}}$ and $\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{co}}$.
\par\exa We characterize monads in $\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{op}}$ and $\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{co}}$.
\begin{itemize}\itemize@item@(\hrefhttps://nmvdw.github.io/FormalTheoryOfMonadsUnivalently/UniMath.Bicategories.Monads.Examples.MonadsInOp1Bicat.html) Monads in $\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{op}}$ are the same as monads in $\mathsf{B}$.
However, 1-cells in $\mathsf{Mnd}(\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{op}})$ are different from 1-cells in $\mathsf{Mnd}(\mathsf{B})$.
If we have a 1-cell $f:m_{1}\rightarrow m_{2}$ in $\mathsf{Mnd}(\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{op}})$, then the cell $\mathsf{cell}_{f}$ gives rise to a 2-cell $m_{2}\mathbin{\cdot}f\Rightarrow f\mathbin{\cdot}m_{1}$.
We define \emph{oplax monad morphisms} in $\mathsf{B}$ to be 1-cells in $\mathsf{Mnd}(\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{op}})^{\mathsf{op}}$.
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@(\hrefhttps://nmvdw.github.io/FormalTheoryOfMonadsUnivalently/UniMath.Bicategories.Monads.Examples.MonadsInOp2Bicat.html) Suppose, we have $m:\mathsf{Mnd}(\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{co}})$.
Then $\mathsf{ob}_{m}:\mathsf{B}$ and we also have a 1-cell $m:\mathsf{ob}_{m}\rightarrow\mathsf{ob}_{m}$ in $\mathsf{B}$.
However, since the direction of the 2-cells are reversed in $\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{co}}$, the 2-cells $\mathsf{\eta}_{m}$ and $\mathsf{\mu}_{m}$ give rise to a 2-cell $m\Rightarrow\operatorname{id}_{\mathsf{ob}_{m}}$ and $m\Rightarrow m\mathbin{\cdot}m$ respectively.
As such, monads in $\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{co}}$ are the same as comonads in $\mathsf{B}$.
\end{itemize}\par\exa[\hrefhttps://nmvdw.github.io/FormalTheoryOfMonadsUnivalently/UniMath.Bicategories.Monads.Examples.MonadsInMonads.html#mnd_mnd_to_distr_law]
Objects in $m:\mathsf{Mnd}(\mathsf{Mnd}(\mathsf{B}))$ are distributive laws between monads.
To see why, observe that the object $\mathsf{ob}_{m}$ is a monad in $\mathsf{B}$.
In addition, we can construct another monad $m^{\prime}$ in $\mathsf{B}$ as follows.
\begin{itemize}\itemize@item@The object $\mathsf{ob}_{m^{\prime}}$ is $\mathsf{ob}_{\mathsf{ob}_{m}}$;
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@the endomorphism is $m$, which is a 1-cell from $\mathsf{ob}_{\mathsf{ob}_{m}}$ to $\mathsf{ob}_{\mathsf{ob}_{m}}$;
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@the unit and multiplication are the underlying 2-cells of $\mathsf{\eta}_{m}$ and $\mathsf{\mu}_{m}$ respectively.
\end{itemize}The 2-cell $\mathsf{cell}_{m}$ is the 2-cell of the distributive law, and the laws are the proofs that $\mathsf{\eta}_{m}$ and $\mathsf{\mu}_{m}$ are 2-cells in $\mathsf{Mnd}(\mathsf{B})$.
\par\exa[\hrefhttps://nmvdw.github.io/FormalTheoryOfMonadsUnivalently/UniMath.Bicategories.Monads.MixedDistributiveLaws.html#to_mixed_distr_law]
Let $\mathsf{B}$ be a bicategory and suppose that we have a monad $l:\mathsf{Mnd}(\mathsf{Mnd}(\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{co}})^{\mathsf{co}})$.
Then $l$ gives rise to a \emph{mixed distributive law} in $\mathsf{B}$ \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{bohm20112,power2002combining}{}{}]}.
To see why, first note that we have a comonad $\mathsf{ob}_{l}$ in $\mathsf{B}$, which we denote by $c$.
In addition, we have a monad $m$ in $\mathsf{B}$ which is defined as follows.
\begin{itemize}\itemize@item@The object $\mathsf{ob}_{m}$ is defined to be $\mathsf{ob}_{\mathsf{ob}_{l}}$;
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@the endomorphism is defined to be $l$;
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@the unit and multiplication are the underlying 2-cells of $\mathsf{\eta}_{l}$ and $\mathsf{\mu}_{l}$ respectively.
\end{itemize}Note that we have a 2-cell $\mathsf{cell}_{l}:c\cdot m\Rightarrow m\cdot c$,
which is the underlying 2-cell of the mixed distributive law.
The laws follow from the fact that $\mathsf{\eta}_{l}$ and $\mathsf{\mu}_{l}$ are 2-cells of monads.
\par We can also look at \emph{iterated distributive laws}, which are monads in $\mathsf{Mnd}^{n}(\mathsf{B})$ \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{cheng2011iterated}{}{}]}.
\par Next we give two general constructions of monads.
First of all, we consider the identity monad: on every object $x:\mathsf{B}$, we construct a monad $\mathsf{idMnd}(x)$.
This construction gives rise to a pseudofunctor $\mathsf{idMnd}:\mathsf{B}\rightarrow\mathsf{Mnd}(\mathsf{B})$,
which we construct using sections (\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{def:section}).
\par\begin{problem}Given a bicategory $\mathsf{B}$, to construct a section on $\mathsf{dMnd}(\mathsf{B})$.
\end{problem}\par\begin{constrInternal}To construct the desired section, we define the identity monad $\mathsf{idMnd}(x)$ for every $x$.
\begin{itemize}\itemize@item@The object is $x$;
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@the 1-cell is $\operatorname{id}_{x}:x\rightarrow x$;
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@the unit is $\operatorname{id}_{\operatorname{id}_{x}}:\operatorname{id}_{x}\Rightarrow\operatorname{id}_{x}$;
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@the multiplication is $\lambda:\operatorname{id}_{x}\mathbin{\cdot}\operatorname{id}_{x}\Rightarrow\operatorname{id}_{x}$. \qed\end{itemize}\end{constrInternal}\par In the remainder, we only use the pseudofunctor arising from Constructions \ref{constr:section-to-psfunctor} and \ref{constr:sec-mnd}, and this pseudofunctor is denoted as $\mathsf{idMnd}:\mathsf{B}\rightarrow\mathsf{Mnd}(\mathsf{B})$.
Second, every monad $m:\mathsf{Mnd}(\mathsf{B})$ gives rise to a monad of categories.
We use this construction in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{sec:em-ob}.
\par\begin{problem}Given a monad $m$ in a bicategory $\mathsf{B}$ and an object $w:\mathsf{B}$, to construct a monad $\mathsf{HomMnd}_{w}(m)$ on $\underline{\mathsf{B}(w,\mathsf{ob}_{m})}$.
\end{problem}\par\begin{constrInternal}The monad $\mathsf{HomMnd}_{w}(m)$ is defined as follows.
\begin{itemize}\itemize@item@The endofunctor is $(-\mathbin{\cdot}m)_{w}:\underline{\mathsf{B}(w,\mathsf{ob}_{m})}\rightarrow\underline{\mathsf{B}(w,\mathsf{ob}_{m})}$;
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@for every $f:w\rightarrow\mathsf{ob}_{m}$, the unit is defined to be the following composition;

\par\begin{problem}Given bicategories $\mathsf{B}_{1}$ and $\mathsf{B}_{2}$, a pseudofunctor $F:\mathsf{B}_{1}\rightarrow\mathsf{B}_{2}$, and a monad $m:\mathsf{Mnd}(\mathsf{B}_{1})$, to construct a monad $F(m):\mathsf{Mnd}(\mathsf{B}_{2})$.
\end{problem}\par\begin{constrInternal}The object of $F(m)$ is $F(\mathsf{ob}_{m})$ while the 1-cell is $F(m):F(\mathsf{ob}_{m})\rightarrow F(\mathsf{ob}_{m})$.

{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}$$For a proof of the monad laws, we refer the reader to the formalization.
\qed\end{constrInternal}\par In fact, if we have a pseudofunctor $F:\mathsf{B}_{1}\rightarrow\mathsf{B}_{2}$, then we obtain a pseudofunctor $\mathsf{Mnd}(F):\mathsf{Mnd}(\mathsf{B}_{1})\rightarrow\mathsf{Mnd}(\mathsf{B}_{2})$.
\par\begin{problem}Given a pseudofunctor $F:\mathsf{B}_{1}\rightarrow\mathsf{B}_{2}$,
to construct a pseudofunctor $\mathsf{Mnd}(F):\mathsf{Mnd}(\mathsf{B}_{1})\rightarrow\mathsf{Mnd}(\mathsf{B}_{2})$.
\end{problem}\par\begin{constrInternal}To construct the action of $\mathsf{Mnd}(F)$ on 1-cells,
we suppose that we have monads $m_{1},m_{2}:\mathsf{Mnd}(\mathsf{B}_{1})$ and a 1-cell $f:m_{1}\rightarrow m_{2}$.
We define a morphism $\mathsf{Mnd}(F)(f):F(m_{1})\rightarrow F(m_{2})$ as follows.
\begin{itemize}\itemize@item@We define $\mathsf{mor}_{\mathsf{Mnd}(F)(f)}$ to be $F(f)$;
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@we define $\mathsf{cell}_{\mathsf{Mnd}(F)(f)}$ using the following pasting diagram.

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}$$\end{itemize}\par For the action on 2-cells, we assume that we have a 2-cell $\gamma:f\Rightarrow g$.
Then data of the 2-cell $\mathsf{Mnd}(F)(\gamma)$ is defined to be $F(\mathsf{cell}_{\gamma})$.
The identitor and compositor of $\mathsf{Mnd}(F)$ are inherited from $F$.
\qed\end{constrInternal}\par Next we show that monads can be composed if we have a distributive law between them.
\par\exa[\hrefhttps://nmvdw.github.io/FormalTheoryOfMonadsUnivalently/UniMath.Bicategories.Monads.Examples.Composition.html#compose_mnd]
Suppose that we have a distributive law $\tau$ between monads $m_{1}$ and $m_{2}$ (\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{exa:distrlaw}).
Then we define a monad $m_{1}\mathbin{\cdot}_{\mathsf{Mnd}}m_{2}$ as follows.
\begin{itemize}\itemize@item@The object is $\mathsf{ob}_{m_{1}}$ (which is definitionally equal to $\mathsf{ob}_{m_{2}}$);

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}$$\end{itemize}\par\par\@@numbered@section{section}{toc}{Eilenberg-Moore Objects}
The second important concept in the formal theory of monads is the notion of \emph{Eilenberg-Moore objects}.
An important property of monads in category theory is that every monad gives rise to an adjunction.
One can do this in two ways: either via Eilenberg-Moore categories or via Kleisli categories.
In this section, we study Eilenberg-Moore objects, which characterize Eilenberg-Moore categories in bicategorical terms.
\par Note that the terminology in this section is slightly differently compared to what was used by Street \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{street1972formal}{}{}]}.
Whereas Street would say that a bicategory admits the construction of algebras, we follow \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{kelly1989elementary,lack2002formal,power1991characterization}{}{}]} and we say that a bicategory has Eilenberg-Moore objects.
Our notions are also formulated slightly differently, because we use \emph{Eilenberg-Moore cones}.
\par\defi[\hrefhttps://nmvdw.github.io/FormalTheoryOfMonadsUnivalently/UniMath.Bicategories.Limits.EilenbergMooreObjects.html#em_cone]
Let $m$ be a monad in a bicategory $\mathsf{B}$.
An {Eilenberg-Moore cone} for $m$ consists of an object $e:\mathsf{B}$ together with a 1-cell $\mathsf{idMnd}(e)\rightarrow m$ in $\mathsf{Mnd}(\mathsf{B})$.
\par More concretely, an Eilenberg-Moore cone $e$ for a monad $m$ consists of
\begin{itemize}\itemize@item@An object $\mathsf{ob}_{e}$ in $\mathsf{B}$;
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@a 1-cell $\mathsf{mor}_{e}:\mathsf{ob}_{e}\rightarrow\mathsf{ob}_{m}$ in $\mathsf{B}$;
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@a 2-cell $\mathsf{cell}_{e}:\mathsf{mor}_{e}\mathbin{\cdot}m\Rightarrow\operatorname{id}_{\mathsf{ob}_{e}}\mathbin{\cdot}\,\mathsf{mor}_{e}$ in $\mathsf{B}$
\end{itemize}such that the following diagrams commute.

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}$$If no confusion arises, we write $e$ instead of $\mathsf{ob}_{e}$.
\par To get some intution for Eilenberg-Moore cones, let us show that every monad $m$ on a category $\mathsf{C}$ gives rise to an Eilenberg-Moore cone.
\par\exa[\hrefhttps://nmvdw.github.io/FormalTheoryOfMonadsUnivalently/UniMath.Bicategories.Limits.Examples.BicatOfUnivCatsLimits.html#eilenberg_moore_cat_cone]
Given a monad $m$ on a category $\mathsf{C}$, we define the Eilenberg-Moore category $\mathsf{EM}(m)$ of $m$ as follows.
\begin{itemize}\itemize@item@The objects of $\mathsf{EM}(m)$ are pairs $(x,f)$ of an object $x:\mathsf{C}$ and a morphism $f:m(x)\rightarrow x$ such that $\mathsf{\eta}_{m}(x)\cdot f=\operatorname{id}_{x}$ and $\mathsf{\mu}_{m}(x)\cdot f=m(f)\cdot f$. Such objects are also known as {algebras for the monad $m$}.
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@Morphisms from $(x,f)$ to $(y,g)$ are morphisms $h:x\rightarrow y$ such that $f\cdot h=m(h)\cdot g$.
These are {morphisms of algebras}.
\end{itemize}If $\mathsf{C}$ is a univalent, then $\mathsf{EM}(m)$ is univalent as well.
\par Note that we have a forgetful functor $U:\mathsf{EM}(m)\rightarrow\mathsf{C}$, which sends every algebra $(x,f)$ to $x$.
We also have a natural transformation $\tau:\mathsf{mor}_{e}\mathbin{\cdot}m\Rightarrow\operatorname{id}_{\mathsf{ob}_{e}}\mathbin{\cdot}\,\mathsf{mor}_{e}$,
which for every algebra $(x,f)$ is defined to be $f$.
All in all, we get an Eilenberg-Moore cone $e$ such that
\begin{itemize}\itemize@item@$\mathsf{ob}_{e}$ is defined to be $\mathsf{EM}(m)$;
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@$\mathsf{mor}_{e}$ is defined to be $U$;
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@$\mathsf{cell}_{e}$ is defined to be $\tau$.
\end{itemize}\par Next we look at the universal property of Eilenberg-Moore objects.
Since Eilenberg-Moore objects are examples of limits in bicategories \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{power1991characterization}{}{}]}, there are multiple methods to express their universal property.
A first possibility, which is used by Street, is to use biadjunctions \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{street1972formal}{}{}]}, and a second option is to write out explicit mapping properties.
Alternatively, one could express the universal property as an adjoint equivalence on the hom-categories.
We consider each of these versions, and we show that they are equivalent.
\par Street's definition expresses the universal property of Eilenberg-Moore objects as a right biadjoint to the pseudofunctor $\mathsf{idMnd}:\mathsf{B}\rightarrow\mathsf{Mnd}(\mathsf{B})$.
This is in essence what we phrase in the next definition.
\par\begin{problem}Given an Eilenberg-Moore cone $e$ for $m$ and an object $x$, to construct a functor $\mathsf{EMFunctor}_{x,e}:\underline{\mathsf{B}(x,e)}\rightarrow\underline{\mathsf{Mnd}(\mathsf{B})(\mathsf{idMnd}(x),m)}$.
\end{problem}\par\begin{constrInternal}Suppose that we have a 1-cell $f:x\rightarrow e$ in $\mathsf{B}$. We construct the monad morphism $\mathsf{EMFunctor}_{x,e}(f)$ as follows.
\begin{itemize}\itemize@item@The underlying morphism is $f\mathbin{\cdot}\mathsf{mor}_{e}$.
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@For the 2-cell $(f\mathbin{\cdot}\mathsf{mor}_{e})\mathbin{\cdot}m\Rightarrow\operatorname{id}_{x}\mathbin{\cdot}(f\mathbin{\cdot}\mathsf{mor}_{e})$, we take

\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}$$\end{itemize}Given a 2-cell $\tau:f\Rightarrow g$, the underlying cell of $\mathsf{EMFunctor}_{x,e}(\tau):\mathsf{EMFunctor}_{x,e}(f)\Rightarrow\mathsf{EMFunctor}_{x,e}(g)$ is $\tau\vartriangleright\mathsf{mor}_{e}$.
\qed\end{constrInternal}\par\defi[\hrefhttps://nmvdw.github.io/FormalTheoryOfMonadsUnivalently/UniMath.Bicategories.Limits.EilenbergMooreObjects.html#bicat_has_em]
Let $\mathsf{B}$ be a bicategory and let $m$ be a monad in $\mathsf{B}$.
An Eilenberg-Moore cone $e$ for $m$ is called {universal} if for every object $x$ the functor $\mathsf{EMFunctor}_{x,e}$ is an adjoint equivalence of categories.
We say that a bicategory has {Eilenberg-Moore objects} if for every monad $m$ there is a universal Eilenberg-Moore cone.
\par Note that \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{def:em-obj} gives rise to a right biadjoint to $\mathsf{idMnd}$ via universal arrows \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{MR2707772}{}{}]}.
We can verify directly that being universal is a proposition.
\par\begin{prop}Let $\mathsf{B}$ be a locally univalent bicategory and let $e$ be an Eilenberg-Moore cone for a monad $m$ in $\mathsf{B}$.
The type that $e$ is universal, is a proposition.
\end{prop}\par\begin{proof}Since $\mathsf{B}$ is locally univalent,
both the domain and codomain of $\mathsf{EMFunctor}_{x,e}$ are univalent.
From this, we get that the type that $\mathsf{EMFunctor}_{x,e}$ is an adjoint equivalence, is a proposition \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{rezk_completion}{}{}, Lemma 6.8]}.
\end{proof}\par Another way to formulate the universality of an Eilenberg-Moore cone, is by using Eilenberg-Moore categories.
Here we simplify the formulation of universality: instead of using hom-categories in the bicategory of monads,
we use Eilenberg-Moore categories.
\par\begin{problem}Given an Eilenberg-Moore cone $e$ for a monad $m$ and an object $x:\mathsf{B}$, to construct a functor $\mathsf{EMFunctor}^{\prime}_{x,e}:\underline{\mathsf{B}(x,e)}\rightarrow\mathsf{EM}(\mathsf{HomMnd}_{x}(m))$.
\end{problem}\par\begin{constrInternal}First, we say how $\mathsf{EMFunctor}^{\prime}_{x,e}$ acts on objects.
Suppose that we have $f:x\rightarrow e$.
To define an object of $\mathsf{EM}(\mathsf{HomMnd}_{x}(m))$, we first need to give a 1-cell $h:x\rightarrow\mathsf{ob}_{x}$.
We define $h$ as

Suppose that we have $f,g:x\rightarrow e$ and a 2-cell $\theta:f\Rightarrow g$.
We need to construct a 2-cell $f\mathbin{\cdot}\mathsf{mor}_{e}\Rightarrow g\mathbin{\cdot}\mathsf{mor}_{e}$, for which we take $\theta\vartriangleright\mathsf{mor}_{e}$.
\qed\end{constrInternal}\par\begin{prop}Let $e$ be an Eilenberg-Moore cone in a locally univalent bicategory.
Then $e$ is universal if and only if for every $x$ the functor $\mathsf{EMFunctor}^{\prime}_{x,e}$ is an adjoint equivalence.
\end{prop}\par\begin{proof}We only give a brief sketch of the proof.
The main idea is that one can construct an adjoint equivalence $F$ from $\mathsf{EM}(\mathsf{HomMnd}_{x}(m))$ to $\underline{\mathsf{Mnd}(\mathsf{B})(\mathsf{idMnd}(x),m)}$
as depicted in the following diagram.

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}$$Note that this diagram commutes up to natural isomorphism.
By the 2-out-of-3 property for adjoint equivalences,
we get that $\mathsf{EMFunctor}^{\prime}_{x,e}$ is an adjoint equivalence
if and only if $\mathsf{EMFunctor}_{x,e}$ is.
From that the desired statement follows.
\end{proof}\par Finally, universality can be formulated by stating concrete mapping properties,
which tell use how to construct 1-cells to Eilenberg-Moore objects.
These properties are deduced from the fact that every adjoint equivalence is split essentially surjective and fully faithful.
More precisely, an Eilenberg-Moore cone $e$ is universal if and only if
\begin{enumerate}\enumerate@item@for every Eilenberg-Moore cone $q$ there is a 1-cell $\mathsf{EM}_{\mathsf{mor}}(q):q\rightarrow e$ and an invertible 2-cell $\mathsf{EM}_{\mathsf{com}}(q):\mathsf{idMnd}(\mathsf{EM}_{\mathsf{mor}}(q))\mathbin{\cdot}\mathsf{mor}_{e}\Rightarrow\mathsf{mor}_{q}$ in $\mathsf{Mnd}(\mathsf{B})$;
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@for all 1-cells $g_{1},g_{2}:q\rightarrow e$ and 2-cells $\tau:\mathsf{idMnd}(g_{1})\mathbin{\cdot}\mathsf{mor}_{e}\Rightarrow\mathsf{idMnd}(g_{2})\mathbin{\cdot}\mathsf{mor}_{e}$ in $\mathsf{Mnd}(\mathsf{B}$),
there is a unique 2-cell $\mathsf{EM}_{\mathsf{cell}}(\tau)$ such that $\mathsf{idMnd}(\mathsf{EM}_{\mathsf{cell}}(\tau))\vartriangleright\mathsf{mor}_{e}=\tau$.
\end{enumerate}Note that the type that expresses that an Eilenberg-Moore cone satisfies these universal properties, is a proposition.
\par To see why we have such an equivalence, we first observe that a functor is an adjoint equivalence if and only if it is split essentially and fully faithful.
The first of these two requirements corresponds to the essential surjectivity of $\mathsf{EMFunctor}_{x,e}$, and the second corresponds to fully faithfulness.
\par Let us finish this section with some examples of Eilenberg-Moore objects.
\par\exa[\hrefhttps://nmvdw.github.io/FormalTheoryOfMonadsUnivalently/UniMath.Bicategories.Limits.Examples.BicatOfUnivCatsLimits.html#has_em_bicat_of_univ_cats]
The bicategory $\mathsf{UnivCat}$ has Eilenberg-Moore objects.
We showed in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{exa:em_cone} how every monad $m$ gives rise to an Eilenberg-Moore cone via the Eilenberg-Moore category $\mathsf{EM}(m)$,
so we only need to verify that this cone is universal.
For that we use the concrete universal properties, and we only prove the universal property for 1-cells.
\par Suppose that we have a category $\mathsf{C}^{\prime}$, a functor $F:\mathsf{C}^{\prime}\rightarrow C$, and a natural transformation $\tau:F\cdot m\Rightarrow F$.
We also assume that for every $x:\mathsf{C}^{\prime}$ the following equalities hold
\begin{equation}\mathsf{\eta}_{m}(F(x))\cdot\tau(x)=\operatorname{id}_{F(x)},\end{equation}\begin{equation}m(\tau(x))\cdot\tau(x)=\mathsf{\mu}_{m}(F(x))\cdot\tau(x).\end{equation}Then we have a functor $\overline{F}:C^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathsf{EM}(m)$.
This functor sends every object $x:C^{\prime}$ to the algebra $(F(x^{\prime}),\tau(x))$,
which is indeed an algebra by our assumptions (\lx@cref{creftypeplural\lx@tilde refnum}{eq:monad_unit} and\lx@nobreakspace\lx@cref{refnum}{eq:monad_mult}).
The functor $\overline{f}$ sends every morphism $f:x\rightarrow x^{\prime}$ to $F(f)$,
which is a morphism of algebras by the naturality of $\tau$.
\par\exa[\hrefhttps://nmvdw.github.io/FormalTheoryOfMonadsUnivalently/UniMath.Bicategories.Limits.Examples.LimitsStructuredCategories.html#has_em_univ_cat_with_terminal_obj]
The bicategory $\mathsf{UnivCat}_{\mathsf{Terminal}}$ has Eilenberg-Moore objects as well.
If we have a monad $m:\mathsf{Mnd}(\mathsf{UnivCat}_{\mathsf{Terminal}})$, then $\mathsf{ob}_{m}$ has a terminal object and $m$ preserves terminal objects.
Under these conditions, it follows that the Eilenberg-Moore category of $m$ has a terminal object.
From this, we can conclude that $\mathsf{UnivCat}_{\mathsf{Terminal}}$ indeed has Eilenberg-Moore objects.
\par For the next example, we write $\mathsf{UnivCat}_{\mathsf{V}}$ for the bicategory of univalent categories enriched over a univalent monoidal category $\mathsf{V}$ \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{enriched}{}{}, Definition 2.5]}.
\par\exa[\hrefhttps://nmvdw.github.io/FormalTheoryOfMonadsUnivalently/UniMath.Bicategories.Limits.Examples.BicatOfEnrichedCatsLimits.html#has_em_bicat_of_enriched_cats]
Let $\mathsf{V}$ be a monoidal category with equalizers
and let $m$ be a monad in $\mathsf{UnivCat}_{\mathsf{V}}$.
Then the Eilenberg-Moore category of $m$ is enriched in $\mathsf{V}$.
From this, we get that $\mathsf{UnivCat}_{\mathsf{V}}$ has Eilenberg-Moore objects.
\par One can also show that $\mathsf{SymMonUnivCat}^{\mathsf{co}}$ has Eilenberg-Moore objects.
These are given by Eilenberg-Moore categories of comonads.
\par\par\@@numbered@section{section}{toc}{Duality and Kleisli Objects}
The goal of this section is to construct Eilenberg-Moore objects in $\mathsf{UnivCat}^{\mathsf{op}}$.
To do so, we start by characterizing such objects via \emph{Kleisli objects}.
The definitions that we use in this section, are dualized compared to \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{sec:em-ob}.
\par\defi[\hrefhttps://nmvdw.github.io/FormalTheoryOfMonadsUnivalently/UniMath.Bicategories.Colimits.KleisliObjects.html#kleisli_cocone]
Let $\mathsf{B}$ be a bicategory and let $m$ be a monad in $\mathsf{B}$.
A {Kleisli cocone} $k$ for $m$ in $\mathsf{B}$ consists of an object $\mathsf{ob}_{k}:\mathsf{B}$, a 1-cell $\mathsf{mor}_{k}:\mathsf{ob}_{m}\rightarrow\mathsf{ob}_{k}$ in $\mathsf{B}$, and a 2-cell $\mathsf{cell}_{k}:m\mathbin{\cdot}\mathsf{mor}_{k}\Rightarrow\mathsf{mor}_{k}$ in $\mathsf{B}$ such that the following diagrams commute.

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}$$\par\defi[\hrefhttps://nmvdw.github.io/FormalTheoryOfMonadsUnivalently/UniMath.Bicategories.Colimits.KleisliObjects.html#has_kleisli_ump]
A Kleisli cocone $k$ is said to be {universal} if the following conditions are satisfied.
\begin{itemize}\itemize@item@For every Kleisli cocone $q$ there is a 1-cell $\mathsf{Kl}_{\mathsf{mor}}(q):\mathsf{ob}_{k}\rightarrow\mathsf{ob}_{q}$ and an invertible 2-cell $\mathsf{Kl}_{\mathsf{com}}(q):\mathsf{mor}_{k}\mathbin{\cdot}\mathsf{Kl}_{\mathsf{mor}}(q)\Rightarrow\mathsf{mor}_{q}$ such that the following diagram commutes.

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}$$Then there is a unique 2-cell $\mathsf{Kl}_{\mathsf{cell}}(\tau):g_{1}\Rightarrow g_{2}$ such that $\mathsf{cell}_{k}\vartriangleleft\mathsf{Kl}_{\mathsf{cell}}(\tau)=\tau$.
\end{itemize}A {Kleisli object} is a universal Kleisli cocone.
We say that a bicategory has Kleisli objects if there is a Kleisli object for every monad $m$.
\par Let us establish some basic facts about Kleisli objects.
\par\begin{prop}In a locally univalent bicategory $\mathsf{B}$ the type that a Kleisli cocone is universal,
is a proposition.
\end{prop}\par\begin{prop}If $\mathsf{B}$ has Kleisli objects, then $\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{op}}$ has Eilenberg-Moore objects.
\end{prop}\par As such, to find Eilenberg-Moore objects in $\mathsf{UnivCat}^{\mathsf{op}}$, we need to find Kleisli objects in $\mathsf{UnivCat}$.
However, before we look at those, we look at Kleisli objects in $\mathsf{Cat}$.
These are constructed via the usual definition of \emph{Kleisli categories}.
\par\begin{problem}To construct Kleisli objects in $\mathsf{Cat}$.
\end{problem}\par\begin{constrInternal}Recall that given a monad $m$ on a category $\mathsf{C}$, the Kleisli category $\mathcal{K}(m)$ is defined to be the category whose objects are $x:\mathsf{C}$ and whose morphisms from $x:\mathsf{C}$ to $y:\mathsf{C}$ are morphisms $x\rightarrow m(y)$.
Note that we have a functor $F:\mathsf{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{K}(m)$: it sends objects $x$ to $x$ and morphisms $f:x\rightarrow y$ to

We also have a natural transformation $m\mathbin{\cdot}F\Rightarrow F$, which is the identity on every object $x$.
As such, we have a Kleisli cocone.
This cocone is universal, and for a proof we refer the reader to the formalization.
\qed\end{constrInternal}\par Note that even if $\mathsf{C}$ is required to be univalent, the Kleisli category $\mathcal{K}(m)$ is \emph{not} necessarily univalent.
As such, to obtain Kleisli objects in $\mathsf{UnivCat}$, we need to use an alternative definition for the Kleisli category \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{univalence-principle}{}{}]}.
First, we define a functor $\mathsf{FAlg}_{m}:\mathsf{C}\rightarrow\mathsf{EM}(m)$ which sends objects $x:\mathsf{C}$ to the algebra $\mathsf{\mu}_{m}(x):m(m(x))\rightarrow m(x)$ and morphisms $f:x\rightarrow y$ to $m(f):m(x)\rightarrow m(y)$.
Note that this 1-cell can actually be defined in arbitrary bicategories (see \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{sec:adj}).
By taking the full image of this functor, we obtain the category $\mathsf{Kleisli}(m)$.
\begin{itemize}\itemize@item@Objects of $\mathsf{Kleisli}(m)$ are pairs $y:\mathsf{C}$ together with a proof of
$\mathopen{}\left\|\sum(x:\mathsf{C}),m(x)\cong y\right\|\mathclose{}.$
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@Morphisms from $y_{1}:\mathsf{Kleisli}(m)$ to $y_{2}:\mathsf{Kleisli}(m)$ are morphisms $y_{1}\rightarrow y_{2}$ in $\mathsf{C}$.
\end{itemize}If $\mathsf{C}$ is univalent, then $\mathsf{EM}_{\mathsf{UnivCat}}(m)$ is univalent, and thus $\mathsf{Kleisli}(m)$ is so as well.
\par\begin{problem}To construct a fully faithful essentially surjective functor $\mathsf{incl}_{m}:\mathcal{K}(m)\rightarrow\mathsf{Kleisli}(m)$.
\end{problem}\par\begin{constrInternal}The functor $\mathsf{incl}_{m}$ sends every object $x:\mathcal{K}(m)$ to $\mathsf{FAlg}_{m}(x)$, which is indeed in the image of $\mathsf{FAlg}_{m}$.
Morphisms $f:x\rightarrow m(y)$ are sent to

\lxSVG@closescope {{

A proof that this functor is both essentially surjective and fully faithful can be found in the formalization.
\qed\end{constrInternal}\par In univalent foundations, not every functor that is both fully faithful and essentially surjective is automatically an adjoint equivalence as well.
This statement only holds if the domain is univalent.
For this reason, the categories $\mathcal{K}(m)$ and $\mathsf{Kleisli}(m)$ are not necessarily equivalent.
However, we can still use the functor $\mathsf{incl}_{m}$ to deduce the universal property of $\mathsf{Kleisli}(m)$.
For that, we use Theorem 8.4 in \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{rezk_completion}{}{}]}.
\par\begin{prop}Let $F:\mathsf{C}_{1}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}_{2}$ be a fully faithful and essentially surjective functor, and suppose that $\mathsf{C}_{3}$ is a univalent category.
Then the functor $(F\mathbin{\cdot}-)_{\mathsf{C}_{3}}:\mathsf{C}_{3}^{\mathsf{C}_{2}}\rightarrow\mathsf{C}_{3}^{\mathsf{C}_{1}}$, given by precomposition with $F$, is an adjoint equivalence.
\end{prop}\par\begin{problem}To construct Kleisli objects in $\mathsf{UnivCat}$.
\end{problem}\par\begin{constrInternal}We only show how to construct the required 1-cells.
Suppose that we have a Kleisli cocone $q$ in $\mathsf{UnivCat}$.
Note that $q$ also is a Kleisli cocone in $\mathsf{Cat}$, and as such, we get a functor $\mathsf{Kl}_{\mathsf{mor}}(q):\mathcal{K}(m)\rightarrow\mathsf{ob}_{q}$.
By \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{prop:precomp-equiv}, we now get the desired functor $\mathsf{Kleisli}(m)\rightarrow\mathsf{ob}_{q}$.
\qed\end{constrInternal}\par Note that similar techniques can be used to construct Kleisli objects of enriched categories \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{enriched}{}{}]}.
More specifically, for enriched categories one can prove a universal property for the Rezk completion analogous to \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{prop:precomp-equiv}.
In addition, one equip both $\mathcal{K}(m)$ and $\mathsf{Kleisli}(m)$ with an enrichment if $m$ is an enriched monad,
and one can show that $\mathsf{incl}_{m}$ is an enriched functor.
One can then follow \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{constr:univ-kleisli-cats} to construct Kleisli objects in the bicategory of enriched categories.
\par\par\@@numbered@section{section}{toc}{Monads and Adjunctions}
The cornerstone of the theory of monads is the relation between monads and adjunctions.
More specifically, every adjunction gives rise to a monad and vice versa.
This was generalized by Street to 2-categories that have Eilenberg-Moore objects \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{street1972formal}{}{}]}.
In this section, we prove these theorems, and to do so, we start by recalling adjunctions in bicategories.
\par\defi[\hrefhttps://nmvdw.github.io/FormalTheoryOfMonadsUnivalently/UniMath.Bicategories.Morphisms.Adjunctions.html#adjunction]
An {adjunction} $l\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.12012pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.12012pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[8.12012pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\varepsilon}$}}}\hskip-8.12012pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[8.12012pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\eta}$}}}\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}$}}}r$ in a bicategory $\mathsf{B}$ consists of
\begin{itemize}\itemize@item@objects $x$ and $y$;
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@1-cells $l:x\rightarrow y$ and $r:y\rightarrow x$;
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@2-cells $\eta:\operatorname{id}_{x}\Rightarrow l\mathbin{\cdot}r$ and $\varepsilon:r\mathbin{\cdot}l\Rightarrow\operatorname{id}_{y}$
\end{itemize}such that the following 2-cells are identities

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{129.76445pt}{2.53331pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
