\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}$$\par Our notation for adjunctions is taken from \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{di2019unicity}{}{}]}.
The two coherences in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{def:adjunction} are called the \emph{triangle equalities}.
As expected, adjunctions internal to $\mathsf{UnivCat}$ correspond to adjunctions of categories \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{mac2013categories}{}{}]}.
This is because the unitors and associators in $\mathsf{UnivCat}$ are pointwise the identity, so the triangle equalities in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{def:adjunction} reduce to the usual ones.
\par\exa We characterize adjunctions in $B^{\mathsf{op}}$ and $B^{\mathsf{co}}$ as follows.
\begin{itemize}\itemize@item@(\hrefhttps://nmvdw.github.io/FormalTheoryOfMonadsUnivalently/UniMath.Bicategories.Morphisms.Examples.MorphismsInOp1Bicat.html#op1_left_adjoint_to_right_adjoint) Every adjunction $l\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.12012pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.12012pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[8.12012pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\varepsilon}$}}}\hskip-8.12012pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[8.12012pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\eta}$}}}\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}$}}}r$ in $\mathsf{B}$ gives rise to an adjunction $r\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.12012pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.12012pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[8.12012pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\varepsilon}$}}}\hskip-8.12012pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[8.12012pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\eta}$}}}\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}$}}}l$ in $\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{op}}$ and vice versa.
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@(\hrefhttps://nmvdw.github.io/FormalTheoryOfMonadsUnivalently/UniMath.Bicategories.Morphisms.Examples.MorphismsInOp2Bicat.html#op2_left_adjoint_to_right_adjoint) Every adjunction $l\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.12012pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.12012pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[8.12012pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\varepsilon}$}}}\hskip-8.12012pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[8.12012pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\eta}$}}}\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}$}}}r$ in $\mathsf{B}$ gives rise to an adjunction $r\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.12012pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.12012pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[8.12012pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\eta}$}}}\hskip-8.12012pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[8.12012pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\varepsilon}$}}}\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}$}}}l$ in $\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{co}}$ and vice versa.
\end{itemize}\par\lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{exa:adj-dual} can be strengthened by using the terminology of \emph{left adjoints} and \emph{right adjoints}.
Given a 1-cell $f:x\rightarrow y$, the type $\mathsf{LeftAdj}_{\mathsf{B}}(f)$ says that we have $r$,$\eta$, and $\varepsilon$ such that we have an adjunction $l\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.12012pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.12012pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[8.12012pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\varepsilon}$}}}\hskip-8.12012pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[8.12012pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\eta}$}}}\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}$}}}r$.
The type $\mathsf{RightAdj}_{\mathsf{B}}(f)$ is defined analogously.
Now we can reformulate \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{exa:adj-dual} as follows: we have equivalences $\mathsf{LeftAdj}_{\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{op}}}(f)\simeq\mathsf{RightAdj}_{\mathsf{B}}(f)$ and $\mathsf{LeftAdj}_{\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{co}}}(f)\simeq\mathsf{RightAdj}_{\mathsf{B}}(f)$ of types.
\par Next we look at \emph{displayed adjunctions}, which we use to obtain adjunctions in total bicategories \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{bicatspaper}{}{}]}.
This notion is used to characterize adjunctions in bicategories such as $\mathsf{UnivCat}_{\mathsf{Terminal}}$ and $\mathsf{SymMonUnivCat}$.
\par\defi[\hrefhttps://nmvdw.github.io/FormalTheoryOfMonadsUnivalently/UniMath.Bicategories.DisplayedBicats.DispAdjunctions.html#disp_adjunction]
Let $\mathsf{B}$ be a bicategory and let $\mathsf{D}$ be a displayed bicategory over $\mathsf{B}$.
A {displayed adjunction} over an adjunction $l\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.12012pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.12012pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[8.12012pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\varepsilon}$}}}\hskip-8.12012pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[8.12012pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\eta}$}}}\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}$}}}r$ where $l:x\rightarrow y$ consists of
\begin{itemize}\itemize@item@objects $\overline{x}:{\mathsf{D}}_{x}$ and $\overline{y}:{\mathsf{D}}_{y}$;
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@displayed morphisms $\overline{l}:\overline{x}\xrightarrow{l}\overline{y}$ and $\overline{r}:\overline{y}\xrightarrow{r}\overline{x}$;
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@displayed 2-cells $\overline{\eta}:\overline{\operatorname{id}_{x}}\xRightarrow{\eta}\overline{l}\mathbin{\cdot}\overline{r}$ and $\overline{\varepsilon}:\overline{r}\mathbin{\cdot}\overline{l}\xRightarrow{\varepsilon}\operatorname{id}_{x}$.
\end{itemize}We also require some coherences and those can be found in the formalization.
We denote this data by $\overline{l}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.99995pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.99995pt\raisebox{-7.63889pt}{\makebox[8.99995pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\overline{\varepsilon}}$}}}\hskip-8.99995pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[8.99995pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\overline{\eta}}$}}}\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}$}}}\overline{r}$.
\par\begin{problem}Given a displayed adjunction $\overline{l}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.99995pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.99995pt\raisebox{-7.63889pt}{\makebox[8.99995pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\overline{\varepsilon}}$}}}\hskip-8.99995pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[8.99995pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\overline{\eta}}$}}}\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}$}}}\overline{r}$ in a displayed bicategory $\mathsf{D}$ over $l\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.12012pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.12012pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[8.12012pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\varepsilon}$}}}\hskip-8.12012pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[8.12012pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\eta}$}}}\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}$}}}r$, to construct an adjunction $\int\overline{l}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.99995pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.99995pt\raisebox{-7.63889pt}{\makebox[8.99995pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\overline{\varepsilon}}$}}}\hskip-8.99995pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[8.99995pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\overline{\eta}}$}}}\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}$}}}\overline{r}$ in $\int D$.
\end{problem}\par\begin{constrInternal}The left adjoint to $\int\overline{l}\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.99995pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.99995pt\raisebox{-7.63889pt}{\makebox[8.99995pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\overline{\varepsilon}}$}}}\hskip-8.99995pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[8.99995pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\overline{\eta}}$}}}\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}$}}}\overline{r}$ is $(l,\overline{l})$, the right adjoint is $(r,\overline{r})$, the unit is $(\eta,\overline{\eta})$, and the counit is $(\varepsilon,\overline{\varepsilon})$.
\qed\end{constrInternal}\par\exa[\hrefhttps://nmvdw.github.io/FormalTheoryOfMonadsUnivalently/UniMath.Bicategories.Morphisms.Examples.MorphismsInStructuredCat.html#disp_adj_weq_preserves_terminal]
Adjunctions in $\mathsf{UnivCat}_{\mathsf{Terminal}}$ are given by an adjunction $l\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.12012pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.12012pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[8.12012pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\varepsilon}$}}}\hskip-8.12012pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[8.12012pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\eta}$}}}\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}$}}}r$ in $\mathsf{UnivCat}$ such that $l$ preserves terminal objects.
Note that $r$ automatically preserves terminal objects, because $r$ is a right adjoint.
\par Analogously, we characterize adjunctions in $\mathsf{SymMonUnivCat}$.
Now we have developed enough to state and prove the core theorems of the formal theory of monads \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{street1972formal}{}{}]}.
These theorems relate adjunctions and monads, and we first prove that every adjunction gives rise to a monad.
\par\begin{problem}Given an adjunction $l\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.12012pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.12012pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[8.12012pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\varepsilon}$}}}\hskip-8.12012pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[8.12012pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\eta}$}}}\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}$}}}r$, to construct a monad $\mathsf{AdjToMnd}(l\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.12012pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.12012pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[8.12012pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\varepsilon}$}}}\hskip-8.12012pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[8.12012pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\eta}$}}}\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}$}}}r)$.
\end{problem}\par\begin{constrInternal}Let an adjunction $l\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.12012pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.12012pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[8.12012pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\varepsilon}$}}}\hskip-8.12012pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[8.12012pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\eta}$}}}\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}$}}}r$ be given where $l:x\rightarrow y$.
We define the monad $\mathsf{AdjToMnd}(l\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.12012pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.12012pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[8.12012pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\varepsilon}$}}}\hskip-8.12012pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[8.12012pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\eta}$}}}\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}$}}}r)$ as follows.
\begin{itemize}\itemize@item@Its object is $x$;
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@the endomorphism is $l\mathbin{\cdot}r:x\rightarrow x$;
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@the unit is $\eta:\operatorname{id}_{x}\rightarrow l\mathbin{\cdot}r$;
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@for the multiplication, we use the following composition of 2-cells
$$\hbox to377.74pt{\vbox to20.53pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 188.8666pt\lower-8.84027pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}

\qed\end{constrInternal}\par Since by \lx@cref{creftypeplural\lx@tilde refnum}{exa:adj-dual} and\lx@nobreakspace\lx@cref{refnum}{exa:mnd-dual} adjunctions and monads in $\mathsf{B}^{\mathsf{co}}$ correspond to adjunctions and comonads in $\mathsf{B}$ respectively,
we get that every adjunction in $\mathsf{B}$ induces a comonad by \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{constr:adj-to-mnd}.
Next we look at the converse: obtaining adjunctions from monads.
For this, we need to work in a bicategory with Eilenberg-Moore objects.
We show that every monad $m$ gives rise to an adjunction and that the monad coming from this adjunction is equivalent to $m$.
\par\begin{problem}Given a bicategory $\mathsf{B}$ with Eilenberg-Moore objects and a monad $m$ in $\mathsf{B}$, to construct an adjunction $\mathsf{MndToAdj}(m)$ and an adjoint equivalence $\mathsf{MndEquiv}(m)$ between $\mathsf{AdjToMnd}(\mathsf{MndToAdj}(m))$ and $m$.
\end{problem}\par\begin{constrInternal}The right adjoint is the 1-cell $\mathsf{mor}_{e}:\mathsf{ob}_{m}\rightarrow\mathsf{EM}_{\mathsf{B}}(m)$.
For the left adjoint, we need to define a 1-cell $\mathsf{FAlg}_{m}:\mathsf{EM}_{\mathsf{B}}(m)\rightarrow\mathsf{ob}_{m}$, and we use the universal property of Eilenberg-Moore objects for that.
We construct a cone $q$ as follows.
\begin{itemize}\itemize@item@The object is $\mathsf{ob}_{m}$;

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}$$For the counit we use the universal property of Eilenberg-Moore objects.
More specifically, we have two 1-cells $e\rightarrow e$, namely $\mathsf{FAlg}_{m}\cdot\mathsf{mor}_{e}$ and $\operatorname{id}_{e}$,
and to obtain the desired 2-cell $\mathsf{mor}_{e}\cdot\mathsf{FAlg}_{m}\Rightarrow\operatorname{id}_{e}$,
we need to construct a 2-cell
$$\tau:\mathsf{idMnd}(\mathsf{mor}_{e}\cdot\mathsf{FAlg}_{m})\mathbin{\cdot}\mathsf{mor}_{e}\Rightarrow\mathsf{idMnd}(\operatorname{id}_{e})\mathbin{\cdot}\mathsf{mor}_{e}$$in the bicategory of monads.
We only describe how to construct the underlying 2-cell of $\tau$.
For this, we need to construct a 2-cell $\theta:(\mathsf{mor}_{e}\cdot\mathsf{FAlg}_{m})\cdot\mathsf{mor}_{e}\Rightarrow\operatorname{id}_{e}\cdot\mathsf{mor}_{e}$ in $\mathsf{B}$.

{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\qed$$\end{itemize}\end{constrInternal}Note that we can instantiate \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{constr:mnd-to-adj} to several concrete instances.
\begin{itemize}\itemize@item@Since $\mathsf{UnivCat}$ has Eilenberg-Moore objects by \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{ex:em-cats}, we get the usual construction of adjunctions from monads via Eilenberg-Moore categories.
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@Since $\mathsf{UnivCat}^{\mathsf{op}}$ has Eilenberg-Moore objects by \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{constr:univ-kleisli-cats}, every monad gives rise to an adjunction via Kleisli categories.
\end{itemize}One can also show that $\mathsf{SymMonUnivCat}^{\mathsf{co}}$ has Eilenberg-Moore objects, and thus every comonad of symmetric monoidal categories gives rise to an adjunction.
\par\par\@@numbered@section{section}{toc}{Monadic Adjunctions}
By \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{constr:mnd-to-adj} we have an equivalence $\mathsf{AdjToMnd}(\mathsf{MndToAdj}(m))\simeq m$ for every monad $m$.
However, it not the case that every adjunction $l\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.12012pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.12012pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[8.12012pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\varepsilon}$}}}\hskip-8.12012pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[8.12012pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\eta}$}}}\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}$}}}r$ is equivalent to $\mathsf{MndToAdj}(\mathsf{AdjToMnd}(l\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.12012pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.12012pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[8.12012pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\varepsilon}$}}}\hskip-8.12012pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[8.12012pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\eta}$}}}\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}$}}}r))$.
\emph{Monadic adjunctions} are the adjunctions for which we do have such an equivalence.
\par\begin{problem}Given an adjunction $l\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.12012pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.12012pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[8.12012pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\varepsilon}$}}}\hskip-8.12012pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[8.12012pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\eta}$}}}\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}$}}}r$ where $l:x\rightarrow y$ in a bicategory $\mathsf{B}$ with Eilenberg-Moore objects,
to construct a 1-cell $\mathsf{Comparison}(l\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.12012pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.12012pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[8.12012pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\varepsilon}$}}}\hskip-8.12012pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[8.12012pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\eta}$}}}\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}$}}}r):y\rightarrow\mathsf{EM}_{\mathsf{B}}(\mathsf{AdjToMnd}(l\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.12012pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.12012pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[8.12012pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\varepsilon}$}}}\hskip-8.12012pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[8.12012pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\eta}$}}}\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}$}}}r))$.
\end{problem}\par\begin{constrInternal}We use the universal property of Eilenberg-Moore objects, and we construct a cone $q$ as follows.
\begin{itemize}\itemize@item@The object is $y$;
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@the 1-cell is $r:y\rightarrow x$;
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@the 2-cell is
$\hbox to176.73pt{\vbox to18.02pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 88.36287pt\lower-8.84027pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-88.36287pt}{-2.31946pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{

\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.$
\end{itemize}Note that the object and 1-cell of $\mathsf{AdjToMnd}(l\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.12012pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.12012pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[8.12012pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\varepsilon}$}}}\hskip-8.12012pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[8.12012pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\eta}$}}}\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}$}}}r)$ are $x$ and $l\mathbin{\cdot}r$ respectively.
Now we define $\mathsf{Comparison}(l\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.12012pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.12012pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[8.12012pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\varepsilon}$}}}\hskip-8.12012pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[8.12012pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\eta}$}}}\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}$}}}r)$ to be $\mathsf{EM}_{\mathsf{mor}}(q)$.
\qed\end{constrInternal}\par\defi[\hrefhttps://nmvdw.github.io/FormalTheoryOfMonadsUnivalently/UniMath.Bicategories.Morphisms.Monadic.html#is_monadic]
An adjunction $l\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.12012pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.12012pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[8.12012pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\varepsilon}$}}}\hskip-8.12012pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[8.12012pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\eta}$}}}\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}$}}}r$ in a bicategory $\mathsf{B}$ with Eilenberg-Moore objects is called {monadic} if the 1-cell $\mathsf{Comparison}(l\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.12012pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.12012pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[8.12012pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\varepsilon}$}}}\hskip-8.12012pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[8.12012pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\eta}$}}}\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}$}}}r)$ is an adjoint equivalence.
\par The following proposition follows directly, because in locally univalent bicategories the type that a 1-cell is an adjoint equivalence, is a proposition \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{bicatspaper}{}{}, Proposition 3.11]}.
\par\begin{prop}Let $\mathsf{B}$ be a locally univalent bicategory.
Then for each adjunction $l\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.12012pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.12012pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[8.12012pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\varepsilon}$}}}\hskip-8.12012pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[8.12012pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\eta}$}}}\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}$}}}r$ the type expressing that $l\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.12012pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.12012pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[8.12012pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\varepsilon}$}}}\hskip-8.12012pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[8.12012pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\eta}$}}}\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}$}}}r$ is monadic, is a proposition.
\end{prop}\par Next we look at a representable version of this definition.
More specifically, we define monadic 1-cells using monadic functors in $\mathsf{UnivCat}$.
To do so, we first show that every adjunction gives rise to an adjunction on the hom-categories.
\par\begin{problem}Given $l\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.12012pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.12012pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[8.12012pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\varepsilon}$}}}\hskip-8.12012pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[8.12012pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\eta}$}}}\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}$}}}r$ where $l:x\rightarrow y$ and an object $w$, to construct an adjunction $\mathsf{HomAdj}_{w}(l\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.12012pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.12012pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[8.12012pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\varepsilon}$}}}\hskip-8.12012pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[8.12012pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\eta}$}}}\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}$}}}r)$ between $\underline{\mathsf{B}(w,x)}$ and $\underline{\mathsf{B}(w,y)}$.
\end{problem}\par\begin{constrInternal}The left adjoint is $(-\mathbin{\cdot}l)_{w}$, while the right adjoint is $(-\mathbin{\cdot}r)_{w}$.
For the unit, we need to construct natural 2-cells $f\Rightarrow(f\mathbin{\cdot}l)\mathbin{\cdot}r$, and for which we take

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{75.77106pt}{3.8944pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\qed$$\end{constrInternal}\par\defi[\hrefhttps://nmvdw.github.io/FormalTheoryOfMonadsUnivalently/UniMath.Bicategories.Morphisms.Monadic.html#is_monadic_repr]
An adjunction $l\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.12012pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.12012pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[8.12012pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\varepsilon}$}}}\hskip-8.12012pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[8.12012pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\eta}$}}}\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}$}}}r$ in a locally univalent bicategory is called {representably monadic} if
for every $w\in\mathsf{B}$ the adjunction $\mathsf{HomAdj}_{w}(l\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.12012pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.12012pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[8.12012pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\varepsilon}$}}}\hskip-8.12012pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[8.12012pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\eta}$}}}\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}$}}}r)$ is a monadic 1-cell in $\mathsf{UnivCat}$.
\par Note that we require the bicategory in \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{def:repr-monadic} to be locally univalent, so that each hom-category lies in $\mathsf{UnivCat}$.
By \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{prop:isaprop_monadic} the type that a 1-cell is representably monadic, is a proposition.
Now we show that these two notions of monadicity are equivalent.
We first prove the following lemma.
\par\begin{lem}A 1-cell $f:x\rightarrow y$ in a bicategory $\mathsf{B}$ is an adjoint equivalence if and only if for all $w:\mathsf{B}$ the functor $(-\mathbin{\cdot}f)_{w}$ is an adjoint equivalence of categories.
\end{lem}\par\begin{proof}Suppose, we have a adjoint equivalence $f:x\simeq y$ and let $w:\mathsf{B}$.
By \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{constr:hom-adj}, we obtain an adjunction $\mathsf{HomAdj}_{w}(l\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.12012pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.12012pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[8.12012pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\varepsilon}$}}}\hskip-8.12012pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[8.12012pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\eta}$}}}\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}$}}}r)$ between $\underline{\mathsf{B}(w,x)}$ and $\underline{\mathsf{B}(w,y)}$ whose right adjoint is $(-\mathbin{\cdot}f)_{w}$.
Since the unit and counit of $f$ are invertible, the unit and counit of $\mathsf{HomAdj}_{w}(l\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.12012pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.12012pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[8.12012pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\varepsilon}$}}}\hskip-8.12012pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[8.12012pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\eta}$}}}\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}$}}}r)$ are invertible as well, and thus we get the desired adjoint equivalence.
\par Next suppose that for every $w$ the functor $(-\mathbin{\cdot}f)_{w}$ is an adjoint equivalence.
For every $w$, we denote its right adjoint by $R_{w}:\underline{\mathsf{B}(w,y)}\rightarrow\underline{\mathsf{B}(w,x)}$, its unit by $\eta_{w}:R_{w}\mathbin{\cdot}(-\mathbin{\cdot}f)_{w}\Rightarrow\operatorname{id}$, and its counit by $\varepsilon_{w}:\operatorname{id}\Rightarrow(-\mathbin{\cdot}f)_{w}\mathbin{\cdot}R_{w}$.
Now we show that $f$ is an adjoint equivalence
\begin{itemize}\itemize@item@The right adjoint is $R_{y}(\operatorname{id}_{y}):\underline{\mathsf{B}(y,x)}$.
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@The unit is $\eta_{w}(\operatorname{id}_{y}):R_{y}(\operatorname{id}_{y})\mathbin{\cdot}f\Rightarrow\operatorname{id}_{y}$.
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@For the counit, we use the following composition
$$\hbox to412.06pt{\vbox to69.67pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 206.02573pt\lower-34.65529pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}$$\end{itemize}Since both the unit and counit are invertible, $f$ is indeed an adjoint equivalence.
\end{proof}\par\begin{thm}An adjunction is monadic if and only if it is representably monadic.
\end{thm}\par\begin{proof}Suppose that we have an adjunction $l\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.12012pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.12012pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[8.12012pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\varepsilon}$}}}\hskip-8.12012pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[8.12012pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\eta}$}}}\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}$}}}r$ and that we have $w:\mathsf{B}$.
First, we note that we have a monad on $\underline{\mathsf{B}(w,y)}$, namely $m\coloneqq\mathsf{HomMnd}_{w}(\mathsf{HomAdj}_{w}(l\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.12012pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.12012pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[8.12012pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\varepsilon}$}}}\hskip-8.12012pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[8.12012pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\eta}$}}}\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}$}}}r))$.
We denote the comparison cell $\mathsf{Comparison}(\mathsf{HomAdj}_{w}(l\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.12012pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.12012pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[8.12012pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\varepsilon}$}}}\hskip-8.12012pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[8.12012pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\eta}$}}}\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}$}}}r))$ by $F$.
\par We also have a functor $(-\mathbin{\cdot}\mathsf{Comparison}(l\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.12012pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.12012pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[8.12012pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\varepsilon}$}}}\hskip-8.12012pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[8.12012pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\eta}$}}}\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}$}}}r))_{w}:\underline{\mathsf{B}(w,y)}\rightarrow\underline{\mathsf{B}(w,\mathsf{EM}_{\mathsf{B}}(m^{\prime}))}$, which we denote by $G$.
We write $m^{\prime}$ for the monad $\mathsf{HomMnd}_{w}(m)$, and recall that by \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{prop:em-ump} we have an adjoint equivalence from $\underline{\mathsf{B}(w,\mathsf{EM}_{\mathsf{B}}(m^{\prime}))}$ to $\mathsf{EM}(\mathsf{HomMnd}_{w}(m))$.
Denote this equivalence by $H$.
There also is a functor $K:\mathsf{EM}(m^{\prime})\rightarrow\mathsf{EM}(m)$ and a natural isomorphism $\tau:F\mathbin{\cdot}(H\mathbin{\cdot}K)\cong G$: their precise definition can be found in the formalization.
\par As such, we have the following diagram for every $w:\mathsf{B}$:

{{{{{}}{
{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{}{{{{{}}{
{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{{}}{}{}{}{}{}{{{}{}}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}{{{}{}}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{}{{}}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -19.97 -16.56 L -19.37 13.53}{fill:none} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\pgfinnerlinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -19.97 -16.56 L -19.37 13.53}{fill:none} }\lxSVG@closescope {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}{{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{0.01985}{0.9998}{-0.9998}{0.01985}{-13.99821pt}{9.77454pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -1.8 3.55 C -0.92 1.68 1.42 0.07 2.76 0 C 1.42 -0.07 -0.92 -1.68 -1.8 -3.55}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@closescope \hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-11.83942pt}{-1.50694pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}$$Since both $H$ and $K$ are adjoint equivalences, we deduce that $F$ is an adjoint equivalence if and only if $G$ is.
As such, if $l\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.12012pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.12012pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[8.12012pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\varepsilon}$}}}\hskip-8.12012pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[8.12012pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\eta}$}}}\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}$}}}r$ is representably monadic, then $F$ is an adjoint equivalence and thus $G$ is an adjoint equivalence.
From \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{prop:repr-adjequiv} we get that $l\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.12012pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.12012pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[8.12012pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\varepsilon}$}}}\hskip-8.12012pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[8.12012pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\eta}$}}}\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}$}}}r$ is monadic.
For the converse, we use the same argument: if $l\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.12012pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.12012pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[8.12012pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\varepsilon}$}}}\hskip-8.12012pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[8.12012pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\eta}$}}}\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}$}}}r$ is monadic, then $G$ is an adjoint equivalence.
Hence, $F$ is an adjoint equivalence as well, so $l\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.12012pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.12012pt\raisebox{-4.975pt}{\makebox[8.12012pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\varepsilon}$}}}\hskip-8.12012pt\raisebox{3.32222pt}{\makebox[8.12012pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\eta}$}}}\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}$}}}r$ is representably monadic.
\end{proof}\par\par\@@numbered@section{section}{toc}{Conclusion}
We translated Street's formal theory of monads to a univalent setting in this paper.
We saw that it provides a good setting to study monads in univalent foundations, because it allows us to prove the core theorems in arbitrary bicategories instead of only for categories.
For that reason, it helps us with concrete problems, such as constructing an adjunction from a monad using the univalent version of the Kleisli category.
This is because one only needs to prove a universal property instead of reproducing the whole construction of the adjunction.
By now the work in this paper has been applied to enriched categories to construct Kleisli categories of enriched categories \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{enriched}{}{}]}.
\par There are numerous ways to continue this line of research.
One result that is missing, is Theorem 12 from Street's paper \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{street1972formal}{}{}]}.
In addition, the work in this setting provides a framework in which one can study numerous applications, such as models of linear logic \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{ahrens:2024,Benton94,mellies2009categorical}{}{}]}, Moggi-style semantics \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{moggi1991notions}{}{}]}, call-by-push-value \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{levy2012call}{}{}]}, and the enriched effect calculus \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{EggerMS14}{}{}]}.
Formalizing these applications would be a worthwhile extension, and they would require one to formalize more examples,
such as symmetric monoidal categories.
Finally, one could study extensions of the formal theory to a wider class of monads, such as graded monads \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{FujiiKM16}{}{}]} or relative monads \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{arkor2023formal}{}{}]}.
